McDOUGALL'S Pategt SELF-RISING FLOUR 1317::EN;r8ioN. FASHIONS OF TO-DAY.

There never, I fancy, was a year when so many dainty trifles were on view in the shops bewildering to a great extent the would -be purchasers of these fascinating odds and ends. It is as well for most of us that Christmas comes but once a year, as the demands on one's purse are very heavy. Still it is a season of jollity without which much of the brightness would go out of many lives. There are so many pretty articles which make welcome and useful gifts. Most girls welcome with delight a dainty slip of net chiffon or some such diaphonous fabric while even more serviceable are the tucked silk blouses. Very dainty also are the turn-over cuffs and collars of fine lawn hemstitched, embroidered or inset with lace, worn in conjunction with a silk knotted cravat. Indeed never, I suppose, has the throat received so much attention as at present und collars of filmy muslin are emphati- cally one of the most prominent features of the modistic hour.

Lace being practically a necessity on every

A PRETTY SLIP.

item of dress lace fichus, collars and lace-edged handkerchiefs are exceedingly acceptable offer- ings. The prettiest wide collars fall Over the shoulders in a shawl-like manner, narrowing to a point at the waist. One can purchase any of these dainty additions of the toilette for almost any price, so that those with only a little 'to spend will be equally as well suited as those to whom expense is a matter of little note. The pretty slip depicted is of spotted net trimmed with lace and insertion, the latter interthreaded with narrow iibbon. The effect is particularly charming. I should advise any reader who has many social events looming on her immediate social horizon to purchase or make a slip of this character to wear over various blouses. It will always enhance the appearance of an old blouse which perchance is slightly soiled, while it adds to the dressy effect of a new one.

Lace as we all know soils quickly (especially in this evil English climate), and as it is not always wise to trust the real article to the oftimes careless hands of a cleaner it is wiser to spend a spare hour in doing it up oneself at home. It is necessary, however, when ironing it to always lay a piece of muslin over the lace as an iron should never come in direct contact with it. This applies to fine lace. The coarser and heavier makes such as crochet, tatting, guipure or Irish laces never really require te be ironed at all.

A LACE TOILETTE.

These should instead be placed on a board covered with flannel and the pattern pulled carefully into shape and every point pinned carefully down and left till the lace is thoroughly dry. Should it then feel at all 'stiff it should be gently pulled or rubbed with the fingers. To cleanse the lace benne ironing it should be gently rinsed in a suds made of lukewarm water and soap. Silk ribbons should also be treated in this way but should on no account ever be wrung or rubbed if you want them to look nice. _ _

The lace dress has by no means departed from our midst and a very charming sample of this mode is depicted. It is of an ecru shade and is made up over white silk. The bodite is so fashioned that by the substitution of a high. necked slip it can do duty for afternoon wear or for a small dinner party. A really good lace robe is a very satisfactory investment, as there is endless wear to be got out of it and in summer time it will do duty

lor social functions such as garden parties sr at homes.

For weddings a lace dress will always look well, but—and this is the most important point --the lace must be of a good quality in the first instance, or else it will prove an unsatis- factory possession. There are some fine makes of lace where the net is covered with a fine floral pattern of sprays or wreaths of flowers, but should one step inadvertently on the edge of the lace and tear it the dress is practically ruined, as even the most skilful darning will fail to conceal the rent. A strong make of lace does not tear easily and is not so readily soiled. Of course a lace costume to look at its best should be made over chiffon, but this adds( greatly to the ex- pense. For elderly ladies a black lace dress made over white silk is most becoming and can do duty at all family gatherings such as are usual at this season.

The dainty tea jacket sketched is of black lace over yellow silk, and could be worn by any woman whether in her fitst youth or middle aged. It is a most comfortable possession, especially if one has—or pretends to have—any claim to being an invalid. Worn over a soft cashmere or crepe de chine skirt and enhanced by a cluster of gloire de dijon roses at the breast any woman will appear at her best. There are some of soune to whom yellow is a particu- larly unbecoming colour, but it is so easy then to substitute as a lining to the lace the exact tone of pink or blue which best suits our com- plexion and colouring. When in doubt, however, always choose a light cream or ivory silk and substitute pink or deep crimson roses for the gloine de dijon and the result cannot fail to be successful.

One can purchaFe artificial roses nowadays which resemble the real rose in a truly lifelike and wonderful fashion and are scented in such a delicate manner as even to deceive the most

The Examiner, Friday, January 2, 1903.

rut (amid lie, say anything at present, et en t wisher, so. I gave her my parcel of magazine S hurried bank to the Langitam. T entered the hotel I saw Sir Georee Duille step out of the lift, which had just descended tom the upper door, lie was alone, and turned into the reading-roatn without seeing me, looking. I thought, solnewhat more pre-occupied than usual. I entered the lift, and Stepped out. of it a minute later close to the door of Lady Isabella's rooms. I was sur- prised, as I opened the door of her sitting-room, to hear her speaking quite brightly, almost gaily, and fully espected to find visitors with her. rut only Harris was in the room, and she was leaving it to return to the dressing-room as I entered, carrying away some dressing-gowns she had been 'hewing my lady. Lady Isabella, for n wonder, wasnot lying down; she sat, in a low easy-chair, her slender feet quite buried in the thick white wool rug that lay before the blazing. lire ; a contented smile wavered on her lips, an almost joyous light ghetto in the dark eyes the turned to me. My heart leaped. Ceuld the 4oetor's reaort he favourable ? Was she to know the joy of living free from pain.? I sat down opposite to her, aid looked the qt.es- tion I could not fraine, She laughed—positively laughed—a glad, spon- taneous laugh. I had never heard Lady Isabella laugh like that ; but it stud mean good news, .and I laughed too. "That is better, Rachel," she said ; "you looked so tragic a minute ago. Well, it is ell right— for me—and I hope it' will be right, for everyone else pr-sently. It is what I have hoped with all strength." I left my chair, and crouched down on the thick rug beside her. She held 'out her hand, and I took it in both mine and pressed it to my cheek and lips. We sat silent for seine minotes, and then I found words. "You mean there is no hope? " "0 Rachel," she said brightly, "I did not expect. such a conventional ohrese from yea For me there is every hope.. 'Wonld the man on the rack grieve if he were told that he was going to be taken off? Would the prisoner in Siberia weep if he heard that his senteng was nearly worked out Yoa know, Rachel, that, to me, death only means- change—the passing from bne phase to •another— that, I have longed for the time to be ripe as eagerly as Gerald longs to go and see the other side of.thit world. Rachel. I could laugh for joy that the 'nours of spy physical suffering are numbered! I pray that the suffering here has done its full work, that I may not need it in the beyond." it so near ? " I asked, :holding her hand more closely in mine. I loved my mistress dearly—loved and revered her. "A few weeks, maybe," she replied ,lightly ; "but very little more pain, Rachel. The worst 'is over, they tell me ; the end will be comparatively easy and peaceful. Let us thank God for that." "I do, I do!" I dared not say more lest I should shed the selfish tears that were brimming in my heart. "I may do just what I like, Rachel; what I feel able to do," continued .Lady Isabella, with some- thing of the gay ahandon of a child. "Lying down will not lengthen toy time, nor will sitting up shorten it. presently weariness will compel me to lie down but when it does not., I may sit up like this, or stand, or walk even. Rachel, I feel gay enough to dance this evening," and then her tone changed entirely, and she leant forward and took my head in her two hands, and added: "I am very very selfish. Yost will be sorry, Rachel; we have been good friends;* you have been my kind com- forter in many bitter hours. But', Rachel, the veil will only be beteween us for a while, and in sleep we may meet—who knows? You have work here, Rachel; do it with your might. My work lies elsewhere—apart, for a while, from those I have loved here ; but apart only for such a little while that when, hereafter; we look back upon it, it will appear but as a day or two." And then she let, her hands drop back into her lap, and sat thoughtfully gazing into the fire, the same contented smile flickering about her lips. She had forgotten me. Her maid was revelling in the thought of the new and wondrous life that was opening before her. Clarified by suffering. The words came into my mind unbidden. How well they fitted her, and.how small the interests in my own life suddenly appeared! I started out of my sombre thoughts as Lady Isabella spoke again. "Do you think Gerald and Irma will maery each other, Rachel?" • What could I say? I could not tell her of my brother Paul's ambitious hopes; but she continued, without waiting for my reply: "I should like to leave my diamonds to Irma, if I thought she would one day be Gerald's wife; all my other property goes ,to him." Again I knew not what to .say. I could, not tell her that mr. Gerald bna,• sold her diamonds, that her bureau held only paste. But Lady Isabella was only talking to divert my mind, and to accustom me to the idea that she was leaving us. She waited for no answer to her aeiond remark. "Sir George tells me that Mr. Densmore thought I was greatly deeeived in the value of my diamonds; but, unless they were changed by the jewellers in the re-setting, I know they are good. I must let Mr. Densmore see them again, and if he is still doubtfsl, I 'shall have them tested by an expert before I bequeath theinas an heirloom." There was a new danger! What if Lady Isabella charged Messrs. Hugh and Clough with stealing her diamonds and substituting paste. What a dangerous network winds itself round and round one false act! ‘lt would be safer to let Mr. Gerald have the jewels," I said, "if you would like his wife td wear them. He and Miss Irma are both very young, and may change their minds yet." doubt if Irma has made up her mind at all," said my lady. "She likes teasing Gerald, and she likes his devetion, and would appreciate his title; but I have never thought she was in love with him; that may, perhaps, come litter—l don't know." "Do you think she likes Mr. Densmore?" I asked. "In the same way only. She likes his money and his admiration. .Unfortunately, girls often marry on that, before they understand what they are about." "Mr. Bathurst will not let Miss Irma do that." "No; he will not let her marry too young. But, with all her childish impulsiveness, Irma is, to some extent, shrewd and worldly-wise, Rachel; and if no strong love interferes, Gerald's 'title may win the day. His desire to win her is the only thing that keeps him in England. He is afraid to leave the field open, though I believe he would have a better chance if he went away for a year, and then renewed the attack. But lam growing very weary, Rachel; too tired for anything. I will go to bed, I think, and you will bring me a tiny dinner there." And, leaning heavily on my willing strength, Lady Isabella passed slowly to her bedroom. We remained in town a full week after this. Lady Isabella seemed to take pleasure in the dim, grey London atmosphere, and in the near neigh- bourhood of so much busy life. "There will be sunshine very likely • at the Chase," she said, "and sunshine shuts out the real life. You may stare, Rachel," she added, with s smile, for doubtless I was looking at her with some surprise. I had always counted the sun as creating life, rather than shutting it out. "I allow that the rising of the sun creates physical life, but it conceals spiritual life ; and I feel nearer to the life that is coining to me here in half-dark London." So we remained. And each day I learnt a little more of the unknown world that, to Lady Isabella, was so real; and each' day I became more familiar with the life that surged for miles around our hotel. The night before our return to the • Chase, there was to he a grand concert at. the Albert Hall. It was to be quite an exceptional performance. Father had taken a box for four, and mother begged me earnestly to take her place. Father would like to have me, mother would prefer to stay at home, and Lady Isabella would like to hear about it after; so 1. agreed to go. I was very glad T did. It was a new experience to me in every way. I felt quite proud of father and Sam in their evening dress; and Miriam looked sweet in pale blue, with a warm white fur wrap—she was growing quite pretty and refined by association With nice people. The great hall was a grand sight, thronged as it was from floor to roof with brightly-dressed people, and. when the Royal box filled up, and the whole vast audience rose to the "National Anthem," I was nearer crying than: singing—it was No grand and, to me, so novel. In the middle of the concert there was an intereat of a quarter of an hour, and Sam and Miriam left the box for the corridor outside. Presently father said he would like to see the house from the other side, where a better sight of the Royal lox could be obtained, and I remained alone, looking about me with keen interest, until a voice in the next box —close to which I was sitting—caught my ear. That gay young voice, and the piquante drawl I .osted so foolishly, could only belong to one person. I stood up, and, leaning my armis on the front of our box, I bent forward, and professed to be deeply absorbed in the occupants of-the stalls below; but

1 managed, 1-efore I sat down again, to give a swift glance at the inmates of the next box. Yes, it was sliss Irma, sitting back to back with me, only that slighi partition between us. The diamond necklace glittered on her white neck, and Mr. Gerald's butterfly gleamed in her hair. 1 changed my seat so that I might, perhaps, see some of the oliar occupants of the box. Yes. opposite to her was Mrs. Ashton, Mr. Densmore's married sister. Irma Was staying there then, and probably Mr. Densmore was staving there also. I could not set• him, but I could 'hear his voice; and it appeared tc: me, from the clatter of merry tongues, that Miss Irma was surrounded with her usual posse of admirers. An elderly gentleman was seated by Mrs. A3hton when I first changed my place, but when, a fex minutes later, I glanced again into the box, he had vanished, and a younger man occupied his chair-6 very handsome young man, with wavy brown hair, and a thick: soft beard, carefully trnuned and pointed, after the fashion of the Vandyke portraits. His face attracted me, and I looked at him again when he was not looking toy way. It was a strong, kindly, as well as hand- so:ne face. He was talking to Mrs. Ashton with interest, and did not appear to have fallen a victim to Miss. Irma's sweet fascina- tions. I rested my elbow on the ledge of the box, and my forehead on my hand, and watehtd him from beneath its shadow, Presently Mrs. Ashton 'spoke across the box to Irma, and as she did so the gentleman beside b r looked straight towards Miss Irma also, and front the shelter of -my gloved hlid I gazed right into his eyes. I knew at once, then, why his face had attracted me so much. It was my brother Paul!

place in a quiet spot surrounded by the cloisters of . Canterbury. Of former Archbishops, only one has found ; a resting place at Canterbury since the Reformation, but several have been buried at Addington—two inside ' the Church—and the remainder in the little churchyard. The surroundings however seem to indicate that many , of them are forgotten, as nearly all the ' graves of Premates, who have been, buried in the churchyard, are in a very neglected condition. Mrs. Temple was untiring in her devotion to her husband, and it was to her help that the late archbishop atteiluted his ability to bear the burden of such a busy When Dr. Temple was tranalated from London to Canterbury, some ladies of the Metropolitan diocese presented his wife with a token of their affection, and: the archbishop took the opportunity of sayiag how greatly she had helped him. She was, he said, worthy of any honour that could be extended to her. "I ought to know," he added, "for I have lived with her for a long time, &Id I think her the one woman in the world.' Dr. Temple also used to say to young clergymen that there is nothing like getting married to improve in preaching, and added that he spoke from experience when he said there was no more accurate critic of a sermon than the wife of the preacher. The perils of the oyster have been once more made manifest by the serions outbreak of typhoid in the Southern towns, which seems to have been due to the partaking of oysters at a civic banquet. The enquiry, a few years ago, showed conclusively that oysters taken from beds liable to be contaminated with sewage matter were exceedingly dangerous, and though no doubt more care is taken to prevent such oysters being sent to market, yet if they cannot be depended upon at a Mayoral banquet, there seems less reason for the public to have any faith in these precau- tions. The greatest care is necessary with regard to all uncooked shell fish, and even watercress and salad vegetables are not altogether free from the suspicion of tensing typhoid. These' and watercress especially, should always be washed in a strong solution of salt and water, as mere rinsing under the tap will not remove the minute leeches, bloodworrns, and other organisms, which some medical authorities are inclined to believe are the cause of cancer. There is hardly anything that we cannot get insured against now-a-days, and apparently the members at Lloyds did so well out of the small-pox scare, that they are now offering to insure against appendicitis. Since so much attention has been directed to this disease, many people have become nervous about it, but for a premium of ss. they can now be assurred of all expenses up to £2OO if any operation is necessary, and the pay- ment of a further £2OO in the event of death. Possibly we may soon be able to ensure against influenza, of which there seems to be more about than the usual winter epidemic, but the difficulty in that case would be to distinguish between influenza and an ordinary cold, or other ailments. The best insurance however, agains all sash complaints is a high standard of general healtht which by regular habits and constant endeavour we can, do much to insure. The winter sales will soon be in full swing, and at these the home wollker ought to do well, for never have remnants proved of greater use than at the moment. For emunple, short remnants, too short for blouse lengths, are almost "given away" yet with neck pieces and sleeves, of some semi-transparent fabric, these make up into dainty bodices. For fronts, and cravats, short remnants too are always useable. Regarding coat and skirt costumes,nothing extreme should be indulged in, however great a "bargain" it may appear, and head- gear should be viewed in a good light. In good gloves, handkerchiefs, and hosiery, there are few bargains to be had. Such articles are saleable at any time. In the cheap makes of gloves, colored handkerchiefs' and gaudy hosiery, much can be had for one's money, these however we should do well to avoid. Regarding under- skirts, and dressing gowns, and jackets, many bargains will be offered, and the same state of affairs will obtain in the evening and ball-gown department, of all the large drapery establishments. The newest dinner and theatre blouses shew a V shaped opening at the throat, sharply outlined with a black velvet band,or piping. Those among us who possess ahopelessly thin neck and pair of shoulders will rejoice at this revival. Few women,however "scraggy" are unable to present a respectable appearance in a V shaped blouse, and this is distinctly more dressy at the present time than the square or round semi-trans- parent deocolletage to which the "unpresentable" have clung so long. Ermine is enjoying a wide vogue. Long scarves for evening wear which twice encircle the neck. then fall almost to the feet, are being built of this fur, and chiffon frillings, and the effect is quaint, and dainty, in the ex- treme. For trimming airy fairy evening gowns, ermine is again requisitioned, and it also finds a place in many of the smartest afternoon toilettes. Modistes have discovered a happier way of utilizing this beauti- ful fur, it no longer appedrs hard, cold, and aggressive. It is now so softened and toned down with chiffon and lace, as to be possible when even quite indifferent conplexions are concerned.

She told me that Sir George wished her to see an eminent specialist, and that after considerable demur she had consented. Sir George had taken rooms for her at•the Langham Hotel for a week, and she would like both me and Harris to go with her. She did not expect any beneficial result, but she had made up her mind to go. Of course, I did not hesitate, and in less than a week we were luxuriously located within easy reach of my people. in Nottingham-place, and of my jewellers in Bond-street. It was a fortunate circumstance that such an unexpected visit shoulct occur just then, for it postponed .Mr.. Densmore's inspection of the diamonds, and it gave me an opportunity to do several things. in town that I desired to do. Before leaving the Chase I despatched my neck- lace by a safe hand to Miss Irma at the Rectory. I would not allow iuself the pleasure of seeing her open it. But I knew that, sooner or later, I should see her wear it. The move to town did Lady Isabella no harm I rather fancied it did her good, after the first fatigue was passed. We both liked to hear the busy rumble in the streets below, too far off to be annoying, but near enough to suggest incessant movement—an entire change from the stillness of the Chase. We both agreed that it was rather amusing for a day or two, but would soon become wearisome. The Chase was never wearisome. I was as delighted as a child to go up and down in the hotel lift, and to use the telephone and the electric bells. I frequently went out for short excursions and amused Lady Isabella on my return with my purchases, and my account of what I had seen. The great doctor called the third day. Sir George came up to be present, and Harris was within call, so I went out for a walk. I had been waiting for a chance to walk as far as the Strand, and I- at once seized this opportunity. It was a cold, grey afternoon, and I walked quickly to Trafalgar-square, and thence made my way into the Strand and turned up Bedford-street. I wanted the office of the Bi-monthly, and I -quickly found it. Yet I hesitated before I entered it. I did not want to pry into Paul's secrets, but I wanted very much to be able to tell mother something of his whereabouts. Then an idea occurred to me, and I briskly entered the pub- lishing office and asked a young clerk behind the counter for a dozen copies of the last number of the Bi-monthly. "Are they for the trade ? " he asked, looking me over with a little surprise. I replied in the negative. An elderly man hereupon appeared from behind a desk. "Can we send them for you, madam? Twelve copies will make rather a heavy parcel." "Thank you, no. I shall drive home if I can get a cab near." I did not wish to give my name o them. "Our boy will fetch you a cab, madam." "Oh, thank you," I said. "I shall be very glad." And whilst the young clerk was despatching the boy on his errand I seized my opportunity. "Can you tell me whether Mr. Paul Howard is in town ? " I asked the polite old el( rk, who was making an ideal parcel of my magazines. • "Mr. Paul Howard ?." he said thoughtfully. "Let me think. We are just sending him a proof if I am not mistaken " ; and he went to his desk and returned with an open envelope in his hand and shewed me the address. Ernest Ward, Esq., Hotel Bristol, Paris. I was utterly puzzled, and I suppose I looked as I felt, for my friend added: "Paul Howard is the name he writes under." "Are you sure ? " I asked. "Oh, yes, madam. His real name is Ward. I am told he has bought the Seer, and means to make it a tip-top magazine." "It is curious he should have chosen my brother's name as a nom de plume," I said; and, thanking the polite old man I paid for my now worthless parcel, and stepped into my hansom and told the driver to take me to Nottingham- place. I felt horribly disappointed, and not a little humiliated. I had read the article I thought, was Paul's over and over until I could almost repeat it. I thought I had recognised himself in every line of it, and I had delighted in Mr. Bathurst's and Lady Isabella's praise of his concise literary style, the outcome, as I said, of years of good "Ss,ading. rpurposely left my dozen copies of the magazine in the cab when I alighted, but the too honest cabman brought it after me, and I had to give him a shilling for his undesired honesty. I found mother all alone. It was Saturday, and father had taken Sam and Miriam to a special concert at the Crystal Palace. They were going to dine there afterwards, and see an exhibition of electric light novelties. "I did not feel equal to so much," said mother, when she told me this, "so I persuaded them to , go without me. Father would have preferred to stay at home, but he thought it better to go with ' the children." "Much better," I agreed. We have been everywhere with them ai yet," said mother; "and they never seem to wish to go without' us." "Why should they ? " I asked. their pleasure instead of spoiling it-." "I am longing to get home, Rachel. But I mean to be patient till Sam and Miriam have got their sea legs, as father calls it—until they are used to money and pleasure. lam longing to get , my own home and my holiday home in order. I have made friends with one of the citrates here, and with his wife, and it, is heartrending to hear . some of their stories of the distress in even these , few streets near us. They say they will be so thankful to send some of their wretched poor to Barton's farm occasionally. I um quite busy • making many things we shall want. Mrs. Butler ' has even interested Miriam, and she has been helping me since her holidays began." "Shall you stay here for Christmas,mother ? " asked. "Oh, yes, dear. We shall stay till the beginning of February, I think; then Sam goes into his engineering works; and we shall very likely leave Miriam as a boarder with Mrs. Butler to attend Queen's College till the end of the summer term. She likes Mrs.' Butler. They ,are really good people, but they don't think it wicked to be jolly.' "That will be a splendid plan.,. Then you and father can please yourselves for awhile." . Mother and I had our tea together, and I told her all about my visit to Bedfor,d-street, and my disappointment, but she did not seem much impressed, or to share my disappointment. She sat thoughtfully stroking a great white cat thr.., belonged to the house, but that found mother's lap, what we children had always found it, the snuggest haven anywhere. We both ant silent for a few minutes. I began to have a glimmering idea of why Paul had reversed the usual order of things and was living limier an assumed name and writing under his own.

THE POETS AND POETRY OF BLACKBURN.

Mr. George Hull has placed Blackburnians in particu- lar,and Lancashire folk in general,under a considerable debt of gratitude by his publication of so carefully- compiled and complete a work as "The Poets and Poetry of Blackburn.". It is a volume of 504 pages, and contains forty portraits printed on art paper—and most of them capital likenesses—of the poets whose works are quoted and reviested in the book. in his introduc- tion,Mr. Hull, himself a poet of no mean order, alludes to the unique position of Blackburn as a foster-mother of poetic genius. He says: "As a large town, it is not nearly so old as many of its neighbours, yet it excels them all in the number of versatility of Its native poets To find anything like a parallel, one would have to go as far as Manchester, and there, doubtless,—through that city drawing to itself the poets of surrounding districts—would our glory be eclipsed. The Manchester school of poets will be remembered for ages; thanks to the genius of Waugh, Brierley. Swain, and a host of others, aided by the scholarly labours of such men as Harland, Wilkinson and Milner. And if our "Blackburn school" be not so eminent, it is at least worthy to take rank close behind that of Manchester as a nursery of native Lancashire literature. Such names as Baron and Rillington, Dugdale, Walker, Rawcliffe, Yates, and others too numerous to mention, suggest to an old Blackburnian a garland of poesy such as very few towns can show." THE LATE MR. JOHN WALKER. The booki of course, includes a representative selection from the poetic writings of the late Mr. John Walker, a native of Blackburn, and well-known in Warrington as the editor and proprietor of this paper up to the time of his death in 1892. Mr. Walker wrote a great deal, and it has only been possible to givea number of his better- known productions, but these include blank verse, rhymes in the dialect, songs and lyrics. Those which Mr. Hull reprints are: "Descent from Scawfell Pike to Sprinkling Tarn," "The Factory Lad," "My Books: A Sabbath Reverie," "Sonnet,written in Chorley Cemetery," "My Grandad," "My Granny," "Epistle to the Editor of Ben Brierley's Journal," "Ralph Waldo Emerson" (In memoriam), and tOde on the Death of William Billing- ton." The selection is accompanied by a portrait of Mr. Walker, and will, we are sure, lend additional interest to Mr. Hull's book in the eyes of many old Warringtonians. We will just add that the book is well printed and neatly bound by Messrs. .Toulmin, and the published price is 6s.

So, when we reached the housekeeper's door I let Miss Irma pass in first, and, leaving her to believe that I was following her,l fled fleetly up the back staircase, and gained the studio thrwigh my lady's bedroom. I found an interested group gathered in the big hay window. Lady 'lsabella and Mrs. Ashton were seated on the great wide couch that usually stood in or near that window. Mr. Densmore was in an arm-chair close in front of them, whilst Mr. Gerald leant over the head of the couch near his mother, who held the miniature brooch in her hand. One and all looked puzzled and excited.

As I slipped noiselessly into the room Lady Isabella glanced up and called to me. "Do come here, Rachel," she exclaimed. "These diamonds are bewitched. We are all puzzled beyond expression." I was too nervous and uneasy to say anything; but I crossed the room and stood beside Mr. Dens- more's chair. He rose and offered it to me, but I preferred to stand, and he knelt with one knee upon it bending closely and critically over that everlasting tiara. "Look,. Rachel," said my lady. holding out the brooch to me. "Do you remember the evening Gerald brought the diamonds home, that we all admired the skill with which the artist had repro- duced his fox-head scarf-nin ? Look at that." I took the brooch and gazed at the miniature closely. For a moment or two I could see nothing, my eyes were dizzy with apprehension. As they cleared Mr. Gerald's face and eyes stood out vivid and distinct within their circle of brilliants. But there was no fox-head scarf-pin in his cravat, no pin at all that I could discern; nothing noticeable, at any rate. Yet, I well remebered our admiring the painting of the tiny fox-head on the scarf-pm. Of course, I under- stood at once that the artist who had copied the portrait for me in such hot haste had failed to put in this small detail. But what could I say? My hands shook with nervousness; it was as much as I could do to prevent may teeth from chattering audibly. Mrs. Ashten came to my rescue. "We are frightening Alps Howard horribly." she exclaimed, in a good-imtured motherly tone. "There mutt be some explanation of such a curious matter." "I am sure I do not know the explanation," said Lady Isabella. "You remember our noticing the fox-head, Rachel, do you not?" '.'Perfectly," I replied, still gazing intently at the brooch. "Well, it is not there." "No it is not here, certainly,", I replied, speaking slowly and thoughtfully, for I was trying to find a wav out of this fresh net. "And Mr: Densmore does not believe ,that these diamondi are the same as the ones I shewed him before," said Lady Isabella, laughing somewhat Sarcastically. "Nay, I hardly went so far as that," exclaimed Mr. Densmore. "I said they puzzled me; and I should not have mentioned the fact that I was puzzled but for your discovering the absence of the scarf-pin. These diamonds are undoubtedly real and valuable gems ; the others I was strongly of Opinion were paste." I "But there are no 'others,' Mr. Densmore," exclaimed Lady Isabella. "I have only these." "Then how about the scarf-pin ? " asked Mr. Densmore. As he spoke I looked up to hand the brooch back to Lady Isabella, who sat with the case belonging to it lying open on her lap. As I did so I noticed Mr. Gerald, who not spoken a word since I entered the room. He was watching me intently. For two or three seconds we gazed at each other, and then I turned away to dispense tea. But I knew from that blief gaze that he had discovered my secret or, at least, that he suspected it. • Would it have been better never to have meddled at 11? I could not say. I turned it over in may mi-d, throughout the night, waking and sleeping, b, •i- when the morning came I still could not say. Our sters here must always be taken in the dark. WOMEN'S CHAT.

NEW LICENSING ACT.

How it Will Work.

Under the new Licensing Act, which came into force on New Year's Day, a simple drunk, whether in a public- house or not, can be arrested,so that the inebriates who may be seen lurching about the streets on a Saturday night, and in all the great cities, will in future be run in by the police. Another salutary provision of the new Act directs that drunkards in charge of children under seven years of age. or who "appear" to be under seven, may be arrested and fined forty shillings, or one month's imprisonment. The simple " drunk," whether incapable or not, found in the streets can not only be fined, but bound over not to be drunk in public again for over 12 months.

Another provision deals with habitual, as distin- guished from casual, inebriates. It does justice to the husband as well as to the wife. Hitherto the woman alone could get judicially separated from her husband If he was a dipsomaniac, and he was bound to keep her and the children; the husband could not get a separa- tion from his wife, however gross her misconduct might be. Under the new Licensing Act the husband will no longer be legally liable to cohabit with his wife, though he will have to contribute towards the keep of herself and children. It will be enough to prove that one or other is the victim of drink to obtain relief from the burden of life in common. Further, the offender with his or her consent, may be sent to, and detained in, an Inebriates' Home until cured.

All publicans throughout the country, are more especi- ally perturbed by the new responsibilities which the Act imposes on them towards. their customers. They will not be allowed in future to sell drink to habitual drunk- ards, for a space of three years; or if they do the publican may be fined £lO the first time and £2O for subsequent offences • and the habitual drunkard who gets liquor within three years of his conviction, or even attempts to get it, may be fined 205., the first time, and 40s. afterwards. Alders and abettors in the commission of the offence are liable to a fine of 405., or a month's hard labour. The licensing justices will begin to enforce the Act at the ensuing licensing sessions in February, instead of August, as hitherto, and they have ample powers to enforce it. They will have their own black list of the offending publicans, and they have "free and" unquali- fied discretion" with certain exceptions, to refuse or renew a licence. In future, by the way, no conviction will be recorded on the licence; a record will be kept by the justices' clerk, and he will informthe magistrates of it at the proper time, as also the owner of the premises. Failure to comply with any order of the magistrates,entails on the landlord a daily penalty of 20s. until he obeys. The Act also applies for the first time to clubs in which intoxicants are sold, and requires the secretary of each club to keep a register of the members, and to furnish full particulars to the justices' clerk as may be required. The police are to assist publicans in the identification of habitual drunkards, for which purpose the publicans are to be notified of each conviction of such inebriates, but it will also be necessary for the publicans them- selves to keep a " black list" of their own, and a valu- able means of identification will no doubt be photo- graphs of the inebriates, which will be furnished by the police, along with the reCord of the convictions.

NORTHERN RUGBY UNION.

THE LEAGUE.---FIRST DIVISION.

WARRINGTON v. HUDDERSFIELD.

Warrington made another journey into Yorkshire on Saturday, the engagement for the day being with Huddersfield on the Fartown ground. The weather was all that could be desired and 6,000 *psople witnessed the match. Both sides were represented by their selected teams and under the direction of Mr. J. Priestley they turned out as follows :

Warrington—Hallam, back; Fish, Isherwood, Dicken- son, and Edmonds, three-quarter backs; Burgess and Hockenhall, half backs ; Boardman, Morrison, Taylor, Roberts, Harmer, Edmondson, Naylor, and Coleman, forwards.

Huddersfield—Rea.dyhough, back; Deere, W. Dew- hirst, Larard, and Kitchen, three-quarter backs; F. Dewhirst and P. Holroyd, half backs; F. H. Chambers, Driver, Littlewood, Furness, Knight, Hirst, Poole, and Naylor, forwards. Warrington won the toss, and Chambers kicked off. Fish replied, and Kitchen got away nicely from a pass of Larard's. Isherwood replied with a fine run into the home half but he was not backed up. Individual play by the brothers Dewhirst gave the home team an ad- vantage, and the Huddersfield forwards dribbled over the line in a body, but the ball went dead. A grand passing bout by the home backs put the ball into the hands of Deere, who ran to the line and re-passed to W. Dewhirst. The pass was, however, intercepted by Dickenson, who ran well to the centre. Faulty pass- ing by the home backs lost them chances, until Larard passed to W. Dewhirst, who gave a grand pass to Deere, who romped over at the corner. Readyhough made a good attemptat goal. The home team played well on resuming, and a mull by Hallam almost led the Far- towners in. From a scrummage under the Warrington posts F. Dewhirst dropped at goal, but the ball went wide. Half-time score :—Huddersfield 1 try (3 points), Warrington, nil. On changing ends quick following uplay the Warring- ton forwards prevented any return. Even play followed at the centre, and then F. Dewhirst got hold from the pack and passed to Larard. The latter dodged and passed to W. Dewhirst. who again made another open- ing for Deere, and beating all opposition, he planted the ball behind the posts, and Holroyd kicked a goal. Fish had a free kick, and put War- rington on the offensive. They tried hard to score, but seemed unable to do the right thing. Dickenson and Isherwood kicked well. Fish handed forward when he had had a chance, and the game was stopped for injuries to Taylor who had to retire. A passing movement by the Warrington backs gave Edmonds the ball. He ran smartly and kicked across, and Roberts scored under the posts for Fish to kick a goal. Result :—Huddersfleld 1 goal, 2 tries (8 points). Warrington I goal, 1 try (5 points).

WIGAN v. RUNCORN.

Played at Central Park, Wigan, before 8,000 spectators. In the opening half Runcorn had the advantage, and a smart passing movement, in which all the three-quarters participated, ended in Moran crossing at the corner. Subsequently Wigan did most of the pressing, Barr and Roust, making several attempts to penetrate the Run- corn defence, but the interval was reached with the visitors in front by a try to nil. In the second half the home team had the best of the argument, Rouse and Barr being particularly prominent, whilst Beesley was grassed beneath the cross-bar, the try being disallowed. Maintaining the pressure, Wig an were awarded a penalty, from which Rothwell landed a goal. Result :—Runoorn 1 try (3 points), Wigan 1 goal (2 points). Teams : Wigan.—Macauley, back; Barr, Duffy, Mason, and Rouse three-quarter backs; Anderson and Rothwell, half-backs; Hilton Collier, Brown, Beesley, Halliwell, Ball, Hatton, and Barton, forwards. Runcorn.—Houghton, back; Moran, Butterworth, Price and Dutton, three-quarter backs; Jolley and Richardson, half-backs; Farmer, Walker, Lightfoot, Gayter, W. Houghton, Woods, and Higgins, forwards.

Referee, Mr.W.McCutcheon, Oldham, Touch judges, Messrs. T. Barnes, Warrington, and J. Edwards, St. Helens.

WIDNES V. ST. HELENS.

Played at Widnes, before about 3,000 spectators. Thei opening exchanges were very even, and for a time the play was uninteresting. The only smart piece of pass- ing that was executed by the home side ended inn score. Lally got possession, and passed to Taylor, who after a short tricky run transferred to Hughes, and he scored at the corner flag. Tilley failed at goal. Half-time score :—Widnes 1 try (3 points), St. Helens nil. On commencing the second half, St. Helens played with remarkable dash. The forwards got possession, and the backs attempted several passing movements, but they lacked finish, and the danger which had threatened the Widnes line was removed by a penalty kick. Peacock was given several opportunities but each time he was pushed into touch. Just before lime Alf Taylor picked up smartly from a scrummage near the line and scrambled over. Tilley kicked a goal. Result :—Widnes 1 goal and 2 tries (8 points), St, Helens nil. Teams:— Widnes—Tilley, back; Peacock, Bown, Rispan and Hughes, three-quarter backs; Lally and Taylor, half backs; Aspev, Power, Riley, Webster, Lynch, Leyshon, Turner, aid Oockram, forwards. St. Helens—Foulkes, back; Cleavy, Appleton, Hal- stead. and Liversedge, three-quarter backs; Hall and Banks, half backs; Briers, Green, Carney, Lea, Mawson, Whitley, Thompson, and Mooney, forwards. Referee, Mr. E. Smirk. Preston. Touch judges, Messrs. Elliott, Birkenhead,und Robinson, Runcorn. HUNSLET v. BATLEY.

There was another large attendance to witness the third of Hunslet's holiday programme of matches, and a close and keen game was seen, in which there was very little to choose between the teams. The wind was so variable that little advantage was gained by winning the toss. The principle incidents of the opening half were a shot at goal by the home captain which only narrowly missed its mark, a clever dribble by Davies in which he only missed a try by an accident, and a very flne bit of concerted play by Oakland and Davie W which led to a splendid try. No points were scored in the second try. Batley therefore won the game by 1 try (5 points) to Hunslet nil. BRADFORD v. BRIGHOUSE RANGERS._

THE LEADING CHARACTERS I

THE HUMBERT CASE.

The story of Mme. Humbert, the leading light in the sensational French frauds for which she and her family have been arrested, is one of the most amazing records in the annals of criminal life. She was the daughter of a tradesman at Toulouse, and at the age of thirteen began her life of crime by forging her father's name to a cheque for the purpose of buying finery and bon-bons. At the age of eighteen, she entered a milliner's shop as an assistant, and it was during this time that she made the acquaintance of Frederic Humbert, son of the Minister of Justice. Infatuated by the girl,. 'Frederic Humbert married her. Not long after her marriage, she began to evolve the scheme which has to-day startled the whole world. She conceived the idea of borrowing money on novel security. Her story is that, twenty-four years ago. as she was getting into a train at Nice, she suddenly saw a man fall down in an apopletic fit. She assisted him, and saved his life. On September 6, 1877, according to Mme. Humbert's story, this man, ,Robert Crawford, made his will, and gave her

. , . , . • --

THE HOUSE IN THE CALLE FERRAZ, MADRID, WHERE THE FUGITIVES WERE ARRESTED. bofery penny he had in the world, amounting to £4,000,000. On the strength of this imaginary will of an American millionaire who never existed, Madame began to borrow money. Madame also invented two brothers of the supposed Crawford, who for the last twenty yew, have been in litiga- tion concerning the will. Financiers willingly lent her money, for were they u,, lending to Mme. .Humbert, who would be fah alou y rich when the lawsuit with the Crawfords wa:s closad ? She woald invite any reluctant banker to dinner. Here he would be introduced to Cabinet Ministers, judges, and high state officials. As a rule the bait was taken. In addition to defrauding people in this way of about £.2,000,000, she ran an insurance and savings bank. This was managed by her brother Romain d'Aurignac. The bank was largely ptronised by the clergy, and by means of giving £l,OOO to "Peter's Pence," Madame received from the Pope a blessing on the undertaking. Of course, when the crash came the bank went into liquida- tion. Ten of her victims have committed suicide.

The son of Duke Charles Edward died in 1894, leaving his son the Grand Duke William Ernest heir to the wealth of the Saxe-Weimar family and principality. The latter was born in 1876, and, like all German princes is naturally a soldier, being a Colonel of the Prussian Guards and holding numerous other honorary colonelcies. By the way, another member of the house of Reuss is already unitqd to the house of Saxe-Weimar in Prince Henry VII. the noted diplomatist who signed the first treaty of the Triple Alliance on behalf of Germany, who is married to the present Grand Duke's aunt, the Princess Marie of Saxe-Weimar. The present match has given general satisfaction on every side, socially and politically, except in the case of the mothers of certain eligible princesses considerably older than the chosen bride.

A.: MILITARY NOVELIST.

Swords are someti es turned ito ploughshares, ,but not often into pens, unless it be to give an account of a campaign; and amongst our soldiers we number very few novelists of any note. There- fore, it makes it all the more interesting when one of the principal successes of the Christmas book season originates from a British officer, who wrote it on the veldt, when on active service in South .Africa. Major Woodgate, the officer in question, was out at the front for two years with the .South Wales Borderers and saw a lot of service in the field under Genie. Methuen, Barton, French, Knox and others, chasing De Wet and De La Rey all over the Transvaal an d Orange River Colony. It was (luring his spare time, odd half houni under dis- advantageous cir- cumstances, that he wrote "Tie Unwritten Com- mandment" (Messrs Litnpus 'THOR OF M"UN."RIWTTF:N and Baker) OMANDMENT

• n scraps of paper The book itself has nothing whatever to do with the veldt or the arts of war, but deals with the wiles of peace and the ways of the 'smart set," and is a trenchant and true picture of an extraordinary state of actually existing affairs. The moral of the book and of modern society is : "Thou shalt not be found out," and Major Wood- gate drives it well home in a dramatic tale, full of incident, lightly and brightly written. Many of the characters themselves are plainly recognisable through a thin disguise; and this adds a piquancy to the whole book, which is arousing considerable interest in bocial circles, and is being widely read outside them. Some of the character - sketching is excellent, while the dramatic sense of the author is vex.) keenly marked in all his situations. It is his first book, and will certainly be followed by others. Major Woodgate, in addition to being a soldier and a novelist, is an inventor and a "strong man."

A GRAND DUCAL ENGAGEMENT.

Next to heirs apparent or presumptive to the thrones of the various big Powers, there is no more important parti in. Europe than the immensely wealthy young Grand Duke William Ernest of Saxe-Weimar, whose engagenunt to one or another of the eligible princesses has been eonsiantly .rumoured. One or two of our English Royalties

It is, incontrast to many royal matches, a love- -match, and has given the utmost satisfaction in the princess's native town of Buckeburg, where she is universally beloved from the highest to the lowest. THE SAXONY ELOPEMENT.

Saxony, and with it Austria, is convulsed by one of the most extraordinary scandals that ever befel a Court; and at the moment, so great is the feeling on the subject, that the Court at Dresden has been temporarily dissolved. The cause of this trouble is a double Royal Elopement, of which the more im- portant is that of the Crown Princess of Saxony herself, who has run away with M. Giron, the French tutor of her children, leaving the Palace at Salzburg secretly and without warning. She was accom- 'ponied in her flight by her brother, the Archduke Leopold, who is the only member of her family in sympathy with her, and together they made their way to Geneva, being joined en route by Giron and Madamoiselle Adamovic, a famous beauty, who has completely enslaved the Archduke. The party put up at the Hotel d'Angleterre, from which they have been carrying on futile negotiations with their various relatives, living in the strictest seclusion— the Crown Princess calling herself Friiulein von Oben, the Archduke taking the name of Buriano, and Giron that of Brussels. What the upshot of this extraordinary situation will be is not yet altogether clear, but it is certain that the Royal House of Saxony will refuse to receive the erring Crown Princess back, should she ever consent on ham side to return, and will endeavour to obtain an

THZ CROWN PRINCESS OF SAX, HUSBAND. annulment of the marriage from the Pope, while her family show no signs of sympathy with her. Ever since she arrived in Saxony as a bride, the Crown Princess has been out of sympathy with the ,whole Court and her surroundings, especially with the King himself. She was inclined to be unconventional and artistic, two things regarded with horror by her father-in-law, as beneath the established dignity of a future Queen; while with her husband she never hit it off owing to his lax ideas of morality. Several times their quarrels have been temporarily compromised, but at last the Crown Princess has taken the bull by the horns and settled the matter finally by an unpardonable indiscretion. The Archduke Leopold, who was in love with the Donna Elvira, Don Carlos's daughter, but not allowed to marry her for political reasons, has for many years been unconventional to the verge of eccentricity, and his decision to renounce his rank and wealth and wed out of his station muses little surprise, though considerable dissatis, faction to his relatives. Altogether, it is a most unusual and extraordinary social impasse.

THE LATE ARCHBISHOP.

The late Dr. Temple may have had his critics aa to his theological and political opinions, but no one can say that he was not universally admired as a man. Without doubt, Dr. Temple was one of the most vigorous, original, and capable of personali- ties of this or any other period in the history of the

CANTERBURY.

Church. There was a certain masterful arrogance about him, very apt at times to wound and offend, and the firmly-cut face and even his broad West Country accent combined with this to give an impression of rather a hard-grained nature. But those who knew him most intimately knew that a brusque manner and a rather formidable exterior disguised to the casual acquaintance a real nature that could be very sweet and lovable, and that presented many high and estimable featbres of character. Frederick Temple was the son of Major Octavius Temple, who was Governor of Sierra Leone. and who fell a victim to the deadly climate of that white man's grave." His mother was a Miss Carveth, a Cornish lady. The son Was born at Santa Manru, in the lonian Islands, on the 30th of November, 1821. At the working- men's meet- ing of the Church Congress held in Nottingham in September, 1897—after Dr. Temple had .become Archbishop—he gave an extremely interesting account of his early days. Ever since his early childhood, he told his working-class audience, he had felt most sympathy with men who worked with their bodies rather than with their brains. "My father," he said, "was a working man—a soldier who at his death was Governor of a Colony; but my father died when I was thirteen, and I have had to make nik living since I was seventeen. I have known what it is to do without fire because I could not afford it, and to wear patched clothes and boots." The Archbishop told his audience that he had learned to plough as straight a furrow ea any man in the parish, and he could thresh as well as any man. The funeral took place is Canterbury Vathedral on Saturday at noon

The Examiner, Friday, January 2, 1903.

MARRIED FOLK WHO LOOK ALIKE.

Scientific; observers state that people who live for ; many years in each other's society gradually come to I resemble each other in face and expression. This is ' particularly the case with married couples' and probably , some of our readers have noticed cases where this like- ness is remarkable. I The" Sunderland Post" prints an interview with a married couple, Mr. and Mrs. Henry Everest, whose portraits exhibit a likeness of this kind. And their

opinions and experiences carry the resemblance still further. Each of this couple has experienced illness, and both attribute their present excellent health to Dr. Williams pink pills for pale people. Speaking in their neat home at 5, North Back Railway-street, Sea- ham Harbour, Mr. Everest said: "For ten years I was troubled with a very bad dry cough with asthma. Some five or six years ago,l when I had symptoms of inflamma- tion of the lungs, bought a box of Dr. Williams' pink pills, and lam thankful I did,for as soon as I had taken , one box I found decided relief from all my ailments. I continued to take the pills, and, except for an occasional cough, my health is now sound. Dr. Williams' pink pills have cured the asthma and the painful dry throat from which I suffered. I have tried many other medi- cines, without relief. "My wife too, has gained wonderful benefit from them. She was troubled with palpitation of the heart., and for years had dropsy in her legs, which were marked with large black veins. Her circulation was so defec- tive that she always felt cold. She used Dr. Williams' pink pills, with the result that she is no longer troubled with varicose veins, her body maintains its proper heat, ' and her blood is in good order. •"rhe doctors said they could do her no good, and that if the veins broke my wife would bleed to death. I have given dozens of Dr. Williams' pink pills away to people to whom ordinary medicine did no good, and in all cases they have cured them." Men and women alike praise these pills for the cure of chest ailments, and for paralysis, locomotor ataxy, rheumatism, kidney disease, impoverished blood, rickets consumption, anwmia, weakness, palpitations, and pain in the back. These pills are a tonic not a purgative. Result :—Bradford, 1 goal and 3 tries (11 points), Brig- house 1 try (3 points). BROUGHTON RANGERS v. HALIFAX.

The meeting of these clubs at Broughton attracted quite 15,000 spectators. From the start it was very evident that the visitors' forwards were the best of the two packs, and the Rangers' defence had a very warm time. The visitors had all the play, and the Rangers were never dangerous until just on half-time, which ar- rived with nothing scored. A sensational re-start was made. Halifax pressed immediately, but from a pack, well in the home "25" S. James got the ball, and sharp, short passing between W. James, Barrett, and Wilson resulted in Hogg getting the ball. The last- named ran finely half the length of the field, when he punted across, and Scott, though hampered by Rigg and Williams, dribbled over and scored near the posts. W. James placed a goal. However, the Halifax forwards reasserted themselves, but thanks to Wilson they were forced back, and Little had to kick dead. In the closing , stages Halifax made a last great effort to score. The I home defence, however, was solid, and time arrived, leaving the home side winners of a great game, which Halifax were exceedingly unlucky to lose. Result :-1 Broughton Bangers 1 goal and 1 try (5 points) ; Halifax nil.

===== 0002601_19030102_art0030.txt | included=true | included =====
CHILDREN'S OPERA. "The Enchanted Island" at Cairo Street Schools.

Short but very_ enjoyable was the children's opera "The Enchanted Island," which was presented in Cairo-, street schools on Saturday evening before a large audience. The entertainment had been promoted with the object of augmenting the school funds, and it was upon Miss Edith Birchall that the task of training the youthful performers devolved. To say that the enter- tainment reflected the greatest credit upon the children and their instructress is only to express what appeared to be the unanimous opinion of the audience, who fre- quently showed their appreciation by warm applause. As the title would lead one to expect, the scene of the opera is laid in a beautiful enchanted island, obscured from the gaze of mortals by mists, and inhabited only by fairies. Should the foot of mortal touch the island, all enchantment ceases,and it is there- fore with consternation that the fairies hear from Pixie "the spirit of mischief "that she has carried a sailor boy, whom she found sleeping upon a ship beyond the mists, from the deck to the island. While the fairies are expressing their dismay, Jacky, the little sailor approaches, and the fairies vanish. Shortly afterwards they re-appear and join in a dance before the bewildered boy who had been wondering whether he had been cast 'on a cannibal island. The fairies entreat him to "stay with them "but he has a yearning for home. Suddenly it is discovered that the enchanted mists have vanished and that ships of various nations are approaching the I shore. Amongst these Jacky joyfully recognises his good ship " Nancy Jane." To console the frightened fairies, Jacky proudly produces a small Union Jack assuring them that his country's flag will afford them a better protection than that hitherto afforded by the mists. A farewell song by the fairies, a promise from Jacky to come again, and the opera closes. The characters. were sustained by the following : Zephyr, fairy of the winds, Lizzie Mellor; Attendants, Jeannie Rudd, Lizzie Pemberton, Sam Hayes, Walter Rathmil; Flora, fairy of the woods and flowers, Elsie Johnstone; Attendants, Dora Johnstone, Florrie Spencer, Marie Williams; 'Nymph, fairy of the waves, Christina Pemberton; Attendants, Phcebe Pemberton, Eddie Hill, Joe Mellor, Willie White; Pixie, spirit of mis- chief, Jeannie Mellor; Jacky, a sailor boy, Bobbie Mellor The voices of the children blended very sweetly in a number of choruses, and several solos were given with good effect. Pretty dances were gracefully executed, and from beginning to end the entertainment passed off without the slightest hitch. The scenery which ha been supplied by Mr. E. J. Gerrard, was very effective and the manipulation of the limelight by Mr. J. S. Hil gwe a beautiful finishing touch to the charming spec-1 tacle. During the intervals songs were rendered by Miss Pemberton, and Mrs. Arthur Booth and Miss Hasel- den contributed a violin solo. The orchestral music was supplied by Miss E Birchen, the Misses Birtles, and Miss Haselden.

===== 0002601_19030102_art0031.txt | included=true | included =====
WORST " ISM " OF ALL.

"What keeps our friend Farmer Bramble from worship to-day?" anxiously inquired a vigilant minister of one of his deacons. "Four Sundays have passed since Isaw him omongst us. I hope and trust it is not socinianism that keeps him away." "No, sir," replied the deacon, "it is something worse than that." "Worse than socinianism. You surely are not going to tell me it is deism!" "No, sir; it is something worse than that." "Worse than deism! You alarm me! It surely cannot be atheism." "No, sir; it is something worse than that." "Worse than atheism? Impossible! Nothing can be worse than atheisnri " "Yes it is, sir; it's rheumatism!"

..... The deacon was wrong, of course ; but there is much excuse for 14 in holding such an opinion. To the victim of rheumatism it seems as if no greater evil could possibly beftill him than the agony under which he shrinks and writhes. "Two years ago," writes Mrs. Amelia Coad, of 64, Reidhaven Road. Plumstead, on 26th November, 1902, "I first. became afflicted with rheumatism. Although at that time a strong woman, the suffering I endured soon reduced me to a shadow of my former self. For months I experiencedacute pains,as though my legs find arms were constantly being lacerated. At other times my wrists and ankles felt as though they were sprained • my knees and elbows swelled, and my legs, became almost useless, so that I had the greatest difficulty in hobbling about, and could only walk across the room by taking hold of a chair or table, or whatever else might be near at hand. Tortured with painj found it impossible to sleep at night, but la v awake thinking of my young family to whose wants I was unable to attend. In addition to all this, my appetite a1m052 vanished,l became so thoroughly run down that I spend most of my time crying. It was while I was in thislmiserable state that a friend of mine advised me to try Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup. That was fortunate advice for me, for I derivet much benefit from the very first bottle. The dreadful rheumatic pains became lesa intense, my appetite gradually increased; and as I continued to persevere' with the medicine the swelling in my knees and elbows subsided and power returned to my limbs, until I was able to go about my work and attend to domestic duties as well as ever. Qccasionally I feel a touch of rheumatism; but Mother Seigel's Curative Syrup never fails to relieve it. It has changed my life from one of. suffering to one of happiness.'

Tea and Entertainment. A tea party and entertainment promoted by the members of the Men's Bible Class connected with the Ellesmere-street Wesleyan Methodist Sunday School took place the other evening. Over 200 sat down to tea. Games were aftervirards indulged in and the entertainment followed. Mr. Jonathan Taylor occupied the chair. Mr. Howard, as leader of the Sunday morning Bible Class, spoke; and later on Mr. John Barlow; Wlio is teacher .of the class in the afternoon; also addressed the meeting. He had kindly- brought his gramaphone, and,,mtich to the delight and' pleasure of all present, gave several selections. Another feature in the entertainment, which caused roars of laughter, was the appearance of the male voice choir, under the leadership of Mr. Richard Scarisbrick. Mr. Whittaker sang" Ring the bell, Watchman," and the choir joined in the chorus. Other items were well rendered, all of which evoked hearty applause. During the evening Mr. R. Scarisbrick favoured the company withseyeral ventriloquial pc rforma nee s, which were well re (lived. The proceeds of the party will be added to the new chapel building fund.

HULL v. SWINTON.

Played at Hull, before a crowd estimated at between 13,000 and 15,000 spectators. Swinton kicked off against the wind, and only a few minutes had 'elapsed before Lewis took the ball from some pretty passing among the Swinton backs. and planted it over the line in the corner despite the fact that he had a couple of Hull men hanging on to him. Preston failed to add the goal. Nothing further was done before half-time. After the interval the play was bright, interesting and exciting. Hull attacked with great vigour, and were rewarded when Prank, who was aided by Goddard, got the ball and made for the line in a fashion which seemed to take his opponents by surprise. He scored brilliantly near the posts, and Jacques added the goal, giving his team the lead by 5 points to 3. Swinton played with great determination after this reverse, and at one time they were extremely dangerous, but there was no further scoring, and the game ended in a victory for Hull by 1 goal, 1 try (5 points) to Swinton 1 try (3 points).

===== 0002601_19030102_art0033.txt | included=true | included =====
SALFORD v. HULL KINGSTON ROVERS.

This match was played at Weaate, before 5,000 specta- tors. At the outsetkive-and-take play was the order. Just before the interval Norris snapped up a pass and scored at the corner. Lomas failed at goal. Half-time score :—Salford 1 try (3 points), Hull Kingston Rovers nil.—The Rovers had to act entirely on the defensive, and Lomas placed a goal from a mark by Preston. The last-named then obtained a brilliant try, Lomas adding the goal, whilst Heath scored an unimproved try, and Lomas placed another ggitl from a penalty kick. Result :—Salford, 4 goals -3 tries (17 points); Hull Kingston Rovers, nil.

OLDHAM v. LEIGH.

Played at Watersheddings. before 8,000 spectators. Oldham losing the toss, Frater started, and early on Leigh attacked. Oldham eventually worked their way to Leigh's "25," and by a clever bit of play by the Old- ham half backs S. Lee scored a try. Ferguson failed at goal. Leigh took the game to the Oldham half, and Hinson, Johnson, and Bennett giving a smart passing exhibition saw the last named player tackled near the line. Oldham nearly registered a second try through 'S. Lees, but Hinson just managed to kick dead. Phil Thomas injured his shoulder, and had to retire. Result: —Oldham 1 try (3 points), Leigh nil.

FRIENDLY MATCHES.

WARRINGTON "A" V. LATCHFORD RANG ERS'.— These teams met at the Wilderspool enclosure on Saturday, before a large gathering of spectators. The match proved to be very interesting. The "A " team had the best of matters in the opening stages, and play had not been in•progress long, before Brooks put on the finish- ing touch to a combined effort by Hunter and Duck- worth. Knightadded a goal, and subsequently kicked a second goal from a penalty. Latchford improved con- siderablyas the game proceeded, and after Bate had placed the Rangers in a favourable position, the ball was taken over and a try scored, but no goal was added. Half-time: Warrington "A," seven points. Latchford Rangers, three points. After the interval play was very brisk. Latchford pressed through judicious kicking, and Knight only partially clearing his lines, the Latchford custodian, Brown, dropped a splendid goal. Fins work by Hunter was the means of Warrington renewing the attack, and Brooks passed to Watkins, who scored near the corner. Play was pretty even after this, and at the finish Warrington had won by two goals two tries (ten points), to Latchford Rangers one goal one try (five points). RUNCORN FERTILIZ ei RS v. The Fertilizers pressed, when a free kick to the Juniors saved disaster. Final: Fertilizers, one try (three points) ; Juniors, nil.

LANCASHIRE LEAGUE.

EA.RLESTOWN V. ST. HELENS RECS.—Played at Earlestown on Saturday, before about 2,000 spectators. The home team won the toss, and Roberts started in the face of a strong wind. The start was sensational, as in the first minute tricky play by Barlow culminated in Leach defeating Lee with a good shot. Aided- by the wind the homesters practically monopolised the plity, and the Recs' defence was kept continually on the alert. Several times Lee experienced a little luck in effecting a clearance. Play had been in progress about twenty minutes before Hobin handled the leather, and. then a weak shot from King was easily got rid of. Twenty- five minutes after the commencement Lee made an awful muddle of a weak shot from Leach, attempting to fist the leather whilst it was rolling along the ground, and Stanton dashing up,easily put on the second point. The home forwards were playing in irresistible fashion, their combination being admirable, and Lee having all his work cut out in saving. At this point, Bartley, the home centre, was injured, and had to leave the field. A beautiful piece of work between Garrington and Stanton resulted in the latter heading the homesters' third goal. Half-time—Earlestown 3, St. Helens Recs. I. On the change of ends Batley resumed, and Earlestown at once took up the attack, the Recs. backs having a lively time. Many times the St. Helens goal was in danger, but Lee's clever performance between the sticks prevented the core being increased and the game concluded with the score as at half time.

OHMY'S " COMPLINFINTS." A Prosecution at Preston.

An unusual case occupied the attention of the Pres- ton magistrates on Tuesday when Joseph Smith, pro- fessionally known as " Ohnly." circus proprietor, was summoned for opening his circus for a public perform- ance on Christmas Day. Theatre and circus pioprietors at Preston are granted licences on condition that their places of entertain ment are closed on Sundays, Christmas Day,and Good Friday. Smith issued bills fora performance on Christ- mas night, and when warned on different days by police inspectors, he replied that he would not hal the per- formance unless the public compelled him. The performance was held, and when told he would be reported, defendant replied to the inspector: "Well, we shall be none the worse for that."

in imposing the maximum penalty of £lO and costs the Chairman said the magistrates had been seriously considering whether they should avail themselves of their power and withhold defendant's licence. If, how- ever, anything of the kind occurred again such a step would be taken.

"The compliments of the season," defendant ironically remarked as he left the defendant's box.

At Halesowen, Worcestershire, on Tuesday, Julia Lewett was sentenced to .six months', and Margaret Lewett to three months' hard labour, for causing a child, nine years old, to pick pockets.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES. Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

There was an average attendance at the meeting of this society on Sunday afternoon. Mr. B. Farrar pre- sided, and in the course of a brief address announced that the Benevolent Fund Committee was enabled to dispense some *elcome Christmas cheer to several of the members as the result of the special contributions to the fund on the pAvious Sunday. Mr. Farrar pro- ceeded to review the past year, and in referring to the losses which the churches had sustained, alluded to the deaths of the Archbishop of Canterbury, the Rev. Dr. Parker, and the Rev. Hugh Price Hughes. He also referred to the losses sustained by the P.S.A. Society during the past year. It was now a time to take stock, he said, and he urged his hearers to consider how they stood before God.—Mr. A. King, of M.anchester.delivered an interesting address based on the text "And I sought for a man among them, that should make up the hedge, and stand in the gap before me for the land. that! should not destroy it; but I found none."( Ezekiel 22c., 30v.) He pointed out that men were wanted to-day who were physically, mentally and spiritually fit to stand with the forces of righteousness. He was thankful to God that there was in Manchester a man like Edwyn Holt who dared to stand alone and risk his reputation, risk his money to the extent of £5,000 and risk even his health, for what he considered his duty in the cause of righteous- ness. Men of like character were needed to-day " to make up the hedge and stand in the gap" against the forces of evil. At the conclusion of the service Mr. B. Farrar wished everyone present "A Happy New Year."

"The Messiah" at Runcorn.

On Sunday evening at St. Paul's Church, Handel's oratorio "The Me,ssiah" was given by a largely aug- mented choir.—The principals were Miss A Taylor (soprano), Mrs. Leathweod (contralto), Mr. E. Worrell (tenor), and Mr. Johnson (bass). All the artistes sang with delicacy and precision, the most enjoyable items being "He was despised (Mrs.Leathwood)" I know that my Redeemer liveth," "Why do the nations" (Mr. Johnson) and "Comfort ye" (Mr. Wprrall). The choruses were exceptionally well rendered,tb:e most meritorious being "All we like sheep have gone astray," "Unto us a child is born," and the "Hallelujah Chorus." Mr. B. Crosby conducted with his accustomed ability, his influence being apparent throughout. Mr. Pritchard presided at the organ with brilliancy and verve, and contributed in no small degree to the success of the evening. The proceeds will be devoted to the trust funds.

CONSCIENTIOUS OBJECTORS.

Appiications for Exemptions at Darcsbury.

The Small-p3x Epidemic.

Two applications for certificates exempting children from vaccination were iteardby the magistrates (Mr. W. Long presiding) at Daresbury Petty Sessions on Tues- day. The first applicant was Ernest Samuel Garland, furnishing ironmonger, &c., Bridge-street, Warrington, who stated that he had a conscientious objection to vaccination, as he believed it would be detrimental to the health of the child.

The Chairman: But why? Give us a reason. Applicant: Years ago I went through an experience I should be sorry to go through again. I had the mis- fortune to lose my father, and it was generally believed that his death was the result of re-vaccination.

The Clerk: Do you mean that your father's death occurred shortly after re-vaccination?

"1-17iiifc;;;C:- ot shortlyT";ft;;:

lite Clerk: Years after? Applicant: I don't know the exact length of time. It was shortly afterwards. Mr. Monks: What was his age? Applicant: Fifty-five. • I consulted a mAdical man at the time, and he said that the most feasible solution of the whole thing wos vai•eination. It was a case that puzzled the doctors. (1 specialists engaged. It was a rare disease.

The Chairman: And wit?: ; .:e evidence before you in the newspapers, and the out break o smallpox through- out the country do you press your application? Applicant: lam not prepared to alter my resolution. The Ct : lam afraid I stall have to sign the certificate, but v.- ith very grave doubts, antl I think I speak for the other magisj-atel on the Bmch. You have a liabil,ty to your own family as well as to the country. John William Howarth, of Stockton Heath, made a appl cation. He said he was the father of three children, two of whom had been vaccinated, and who bad suffered exceedingly, and had grown up to be weik and puny. He was of opinion that their condicion was due to vaccination. The third child, who had not been vaccinated, SVCS a strong and robust girl. In these circumstances he had a conscientious objection to vaccination.

The Ctutirman: Do you blame vaccination or the doctor? • •

Applicant: The children were vaccinated from what is called pure lymph. The Chairman : It is under the same feeling that we sign the certificate. We believe you are mistaken.

Children Entertained.

Lost Friday 430 children partook of an excellen tea in the lower room at the Ragged School. The Y were afterwards entertained in .the upper room to concert by the members of the Band of Hope. A comic sketch "The Manager's Dilemma," given by the teachers, caused roars of laughter. The rooms had been tastefully decorated with evergreens, &c., sent by Mr. Crosfield:(Walton Lea) and Mr. J. Charlton. Parr. Christmas cards were also sent by Mrs. F. J. Dell. The Committee beg to acknowledge with thanks the above gifts, and also the gifts of provisions by the tradespeople. At the close of the concert each child was given an orange and a - present off tha- Christmas tree.
