===== 0002601_19030102_art0072.txt | included=true | included =====
THE EXAIVIINEK.

JANUA.RY 2nd. 1903.

How many of us, in The Old Customs andmaking our " strenuous " the New Year. resolutions for the New Year, are aware of the ancestry of the term, or of its curious significance? The Romans sacrificed ,on New Year's Day to Janus a cake of sifted meal, 'with salt, incense, and wine; all the mechanics began something in their art or trade; and men of letters did the same in respect of books and Poems. The history of New Year's gifts is traced 'back to Titus Tatius, who authorised the pre- .seatttion on that day of branches cut from a .wc,ood dedicated to Strenia, the Goddess of otrength. The cake and the wine led up to the Scotchcarousals of last night while from the mystic cutting of the tress and starting of fresh pieces of work,have evolved the French custom o f exchanging -cally Eng Ugh etrennes, and our own characteristi- habit of claiming at the beginning of the New Year fresh accessions of virtue—even if we have them not. Those men of letters prepared the way for our modern diaries—fruit which gener- ally perishes in the blossom if any emerges from the green leaves. Outside tht)ir commercial use, diaries, in fact,are apt to excite cynicism nowadays. 'Even MARK TWAIN, who ordinarily avoids the "b3aten track, has not been able to leave them alone. All tli!s feverish diNgence to restore the years that the locusts have eaten,would be merely amusing were it not for a psychological fact. Our energies, as Bain has reminded us in his work on "The Emotions and the Will," work intermitently• .Attection cannot be sustained beyonda limited period; but it is nevertheless true that our actions may by cultivation become almost rhythmic. If we could reduce the spasm and retain a measure of the enthusiasm we should make our careers much easier. A late PRIME MINISTER, who had some knowledge of stress, held that nothing is "0 much a habit as hard work. If he had added "except perhaps sloth," his observation would have been properly balanced and true. The tital stocktaking and the cleaning of the win- , 121-‘9B of the understanding is therefore not to be den .-reented,although some people bring upon them- 'selves ridicule by their action. ELIA had reminded .us that every man has two birthdays. The one Which in an especiel manner each of us terms " his," is allowed to pass out of notice as the age. of the individual advances; but the other has an -interest "too wide to be pietermitted by king or cobbler." It marks the beginning of a fresh 'reckoning. The bankers rule down the vacant space on the current: rages of our tpass-books to open the account again at the top of another, and such tradesmen's bills as we (or they) may have allowed to run, find theiridatural termini. There -can be no doubt about the break. So much for the individual The chronicle of the year just closed cover our Imperial position. Much responsibility will devolve on T.r. .4 LANs- DOWNS during the next twelve months, not only as regards the Ver_ezuelan entanglement, but many other problems which may at any moment .be troublesome. We have only to reflect on the .rapidity with which the crisis in Morocco has ,occarred to aepreciate how alert a Foreign Seeri.

tary needs to be when responsible for safeguarding the interests of such vast and varied dominions as our own. Lord LANSDOWNE is, fortunately, one ot the astutest and yet most painstaking members of the Cabinet, and we await his explanation of the German embroilment with interest. Then there is Mr. BRODRICK'S Army Reform Scheme to be brought into effective operation. Expert and civilian seem alike inclined to condemn it with faint praise, and its fate will be ascertainable if not actually determined in the corning year. LORD SELBORNE'S new scheme for instruction and promotion in the Navy should be a memorable event. Then there is the South African question, about which in various ways Mr. CHAMBERLAIN is certain to be effected by this tour. And not less important in its effect alike on Church and State will be the final decision of the Nonconformists as, to their method of receiving the new Education Act—whether by passive re- sistance to the rate, to which many at present are inclined to pledge themselves; or by agitation wherever and whenever the electors cm: be touched. , Social reformers,too, will eagerly watch the opera- tion of that mild measure the Licensing Act, whereby the habitual drunkard is to sutler some chock, bogus clubs are struck at (whether they will be hit is another matter), and married people are given greater power to protect theinselve; against the Inferno of a home of drunkenness. There is another class of workers who take very little heed of the arbitrary divisions of time into periods of twelve months, who may yet have it in their minds to make the year 1903 memorable. These are the scientists—the men who labour ohne eile ohne weile. We know the many years of patient investigation which preceded DAUWIN'S revelation. Some of these men have been toiling in dark channels for a long while, with here and there a gleam of sunshine, and the moment may soon be when they will emerge radiant in the full light of day, prepared to submit their conclu- sions ta the fullest examinations. We are thinking especially of their investigations as to that scourge of humanity, cancer. Toe KING has given the movement to trace out its origin a great impetus, and with the advance of bacteriology in recent years it may be that the truth will be realised at any time. Then there is the development of locomotion. The motor-car has still to be made available even for the middle class, and dirigeable balloons ' represent only the chrysalis stage of the flying machine. They have taught us much, however, in regard to aerial navi- gation. These are only three of the interesting subjects with which men of genius are wrestling; , others may be feeling their way along paths en- tirely of their own making. It may be a dull time for poetry and literature, but the rising generation is on the tiptoe of expectancy for revelation of the triumphs of laboratory and work- shop. Something should come this year.

Death has removed a venerable Warringtoo 3itizen in the person of Mr. Joseph Harrison, J.P. He had reached the ripe old age of four score years, and in the closing stages of this long career he experienced the truth of the Psalmist's words that his strength was but "labour and sorrow." The malady from which he suffered kept him a prisoner in his house for three years, two years of which he had been confined to his bedroom. For some time previously he had experienced great difficulty in getting about, and it was often pain- ful to witness his efforts in ascending the steps of the Town Hall or the old Police Station, but he was not a man easily beaten, and he attended to his public duties as long as he could hobble about. He served the town for nearly 40 years and at one time or other occu- pied almost every public office. The Town Council honoured him by thrice electing him Mayor of the Borough and at the Jubilee of the Corporation,he was one of the townsmen cl,osen for signal honour by haviug the freedom of the Borough conferred upon him. Not only did he take a part in muni- cipal affairs but his interests extended to the administration of the Poor Law ana he served his townsmen usefully in the capacity of Chair- man of the Board ot Guardians He was regular in his attendance on the Borough Bench and al- though he took what was perhaps a very extended view of what his duties as a magistrate really were, still he endeavoured to dispense justice with mercy. It is three years since he dropped out of public life, but there are many who have interest- ing recollections of him.

The Spiritualists At a meeting of the Spiritualist Society, on Sunday afternoon Mr, J. Pemberton introduced the subject of "Mesmerism and hypnotism" for discussion, and in, the evening gave an address oa " Fear, faith and fact." At the close of the evening service the annual meeting of members was held, when a vote of thanks •was accorded to the officers and committ?e for their services dur;ng the past year. The election of officers &c., for the ensuing .year rasulted as follows :—Pre- sident, Mr. Stout ; vice-presidents, Messrs. J. Pem- berton, T. Hardman and Moorey ; secretary, Mr. W, Massey ; treasurer, Mr. Hardman; correspondk!ig secretary, Mr, Manseur ; organist,Mr. Forster ; assis- tant organist, Mr. Joseph Forster; committee, Mes- dames Gal lop and Hardman, Miss Annie Furness, and Messrs. Unsworth and T. Davies. The following officers of the Lyceum, who are ex-officio members of the committee, were also elected Conductor, Mr. W. Massey; secretary, Mr, Herbert Foster, and treasurer, Mr. S. Moorey,—On Monday evening. Mr:. NOTES BY THE WAY AT

EARLESTOWN.

FOOTBALL.

On Christmas Day, Earlestown should have played a round of the Rawcliffe—Chorley Charity Cup with Bacup, but although there were plenty of spectators, Bacup did not turn up at the appointed time. Two players, who lived somewhere in the district, put in an appearance, but they were unable to give any informa- tion as to the whereabouts of the remainder of the players. About four o'clock, the latter turned up in cabs, having driven all the way from Manchester. By this time the spectators had dispersed, and it was not possible at that late hour to proceed with the game. Of course this must be a loss to Earlestown, but whether Bacup can be held liable for any portion,is a question to be decided later.

Bacup will brtve another try to reach Earlestown to-day (Friday), when it is expected the tie will be decided.

On Saturday the time honoured rivals of Earlestown, St. Helens Recs. visited the Earlestown ground, and there was a big crowd of spectators lo welcome them. Unfortunately the Rees: were not at full strength, being two of their best men short. Earlestown tried a new player named Garrington, but he did not prove a valu- able acquisition, but. he may have been troubled with nervovsness. Anyway another trial would do no harm. Earlestown played a magnificent game, and completfly non-plussed the Recs., who had to own defeat by 3 goals to 1.

Newton had a journey to Birkenhead to play Tran- mere Rovers in the Combination, but the game ended in a bloodless draw.

The Essex police on Tuesday afternoon discovered buried in the garden of a private house at Ley-ton the bodies of a man, woman, and child, who, it is believed have been murdered recently. Their identity is not yet known.

MOTHER'S MADNESS AT RUNCORN.

At noon on Thursday a tragic affair occurred on the Bridgewater Canal at Runcorn. A woman named Done, wife of William Done, chemical labourer, Penketh-lane, -jumped into the canal with a twelve months' baby in her arms. She was observed by Joseph Harrison,of Tar- buck. Widnes. an employ 6 of the Postal Telegraphs,who promptly jumpA into the water,and, assisted by George Hamer, a boatman, rescued both mother and child. The worirm was taken home, and is apparently little the worse for her immersion.

An exciting accident occurred in flalton-road, Runcorn; at noon on Wednesday. A motor-car driven by Mr, Carter, of Great Budworth near Northwich, who was accompanied by his mother collided withla coal lorry belonging to Mr Baker. The two vehicles became jammed, and Mrs. Carter was thrown; from the car against the boundary wall of the road. She was attended by Dr. Carruthers. She is suffering from an injury to the shoulder and shock. Afterwards she was able to resume her journey to Parr's Bank, Runcorn, where her son is manager. The motor-car was damaged.

The result of the effort should be a substantial addition to the new church fund. PUGILISTIC POLES AT WIDNES.

Two Poles, William Smith and Joseph Kolita, and a Polish woman named Jennie Miller, were brought Up at Widnes Police Court on Monday on remand, charged with unlawfully and maliciously wounding another Pole named Joseph Kutchlanski, with intent to do him grevious bodily harm. It appears that the injured mcm lodged with a man named Wasalouski, at 13, Cromwell-street, and along with others were keeping up Christmas. The three prisoners joined in, although strangers, and shared in the drinking. Kolita quarrelled with the injured man, and adjourned into the street to fight it out. Mrs. Miller brought out a poker, and joined in the melee. Kolita seized the weapon from her, and dealt his opponent several blows on the head, causing blood to flow freely. Smith also struck Kutchlauski a heavy blow on the head. Ho was carried into the house, and attended by Dr. Hatahinson, who found several wounds on the head and One on the forehead. Dr. Hutchinson, in his evidence, said his patient was toe( weak to leave the hospital, and it would be Monday next before he would b 3 able to appear. Snperintendent Keighley asked for a remand for a week, which was granted. Thewoman Miller was allowed out on bail in two sums of £lO, the men being detained in custody.

RUNCORN NOTES.

[Br A CORRESPONDENT].

Smallpox has actually passed through Runcorn and district. At St.Helers a' man, suffcling from this dread- ful disease, was found to have lived for a day or two at Weston. Happily the officials dealt with the case with decision and vigour. Thanks to those connected with the Urban Council and the Rural Council all the persons who C 91113 in contact:with the diseased individual Fave been vaccinated, the clothes and 'bedding destroyed, and the hospital put into a state ofreadiness to cope with any outbreak.

At the next meeting we shall bgs acquainted with the scheme of the committee appointed to consider the alteration of the day for the Council meeting. Tbere appears to be a unanimous feeling in favour of an alter- ation of the day for the Council. There appe Ars to he a uuanimous feeling,in favour of en alteration of the time of meeting though no one seems willing to put in an extra day at the Workhouse, noteven to tiansact public business. No doubt the scheme will go as far as possible and will solve what is at present a real difficulty.

There is every reason to believe that Christm-s day at the workhouse was spent in right royal fashion by the imrates. Thanks to the generosity of several )arge hearted rrsons the day was made, if not into a "feast of reason and a flow of soul," at least into asifmerry and l-lppy a time as possible. Mr. Samuel Tay)or. in his usual bountiful manner, distributed to Over 200 persons a himdredweight of coal each, while Mr. James Taylor an old member of the Board, sent £5 for the inmates of the "House."

RUNCORN SAVINGS BANK. Annual Meeting.

The annual meeting of the trustees and managers, of the Rnncom Savings Bank was held on Tuesday Mr. E. Imison presiding. Present Messrs. NV. Fisher, NV. Hankinson, J. Pritchard, J. Sommerfleld, J. Pennington, W. Sutton, J. Lea, W. Handley (actuary), C. Handley, (assistant actuary). Letters of regret for absence were read from Messrs. T. Wright, J. Barton, J. M. Frith, A. R. Norman, and J. R. Frith. THE REPORT.-SATISFACTORY PROGRESS. _

The Actuary produced the ceraficate of the National Debt Commissioners, the treasurers bank book and the auditors report which showed that the books were well and worthily kept, clearly showing the amount due to each depositor. The Actuary then presented the 60th annual report,in which he said :—"The bank continues to maintain its position and to make steady progress. The amount standing to the credit of depositors at the close of the year was £42,324 3a. 4d. as compared with £41,536 3s. id. at the close of 1901 an increase of £7BB Os. 3d. The amount of consol stock held for depositors and for which they hold ceitificates direct from the National Debt Commissioners was of the nominal value of 26,475. Adding to the ledger balance the present value of con- sol stock at 93 per cent. £6,021 155., gives a total of £48,345 18s. 4d., as the total aggregate of depositors in- vestments. The separate transactions over the counter have numbered 4,065 as follows ;-2,670 individual de- posits, £12,215 2s. Bd.; 1,212 individual withdrawals, £11,6i5 Bs. 5d.;.11 purchases of consol stock, £1,211 13s. ; 5 sales of stock p-zoducing, 230785. id.; 167 stock dividends credited to depositors £159 2s. 4d., a total turnover of £25,547 14s. 6d. A satisfactory feature of the year's working, to some extent showing the grow- ing improvement of trade and employment in the town and district is the fact that the amount withdrawn has been less by £2,630 Is. id. than in the previous year. The lowprice of consol stock induced several depositors to invest in these securities, and at the close of the bank year the amount held showed an in- crease in nominal value of £975. To the depositors accounts has been added the sum of -1 £1,131 10s. 11d. inclusive of dividends on I stock, being an increase of £23 7s. 9d., as compared with 1901. As previously stated the amount owing to de- positors on November 20th last was £l2, 324 3s. 4d., to meet which the National Debt Commissioners as certi- fied by them held £42,231 ss. 5d., and there was in the treasurer's hands as shown by the Bank pass book and certified by him £259 10s. 9d., which together showed a surplus over all debts and liabilities of £166 12s. 10d., an increase over last year of £9 19s. 10d. The auditors have throughout the year kept up a continuous audit. They have also compared a large number of depositors books with the Bank ledger for which purpose they specially attand each thee the Bank is open in the month of February. Their certificate as to their audit and the correctness of the accounts is also submitted for yolir information. The inspector appoint- ed under the Savings Bank Act has made two audits' one in the early part of the year and the second in the late autumn, and expressed himself as satisfied with and approving of the management of the Bank. The number of open accounts, exclusive of consol stockart the close of the year was 1.211, being practically the same num- ber as that at the close of the year. As all the funds of the Bank excepting the small !amount necessary to be kept in hand by the treasurer for current needs are invested with the Committee for the Reduction of the National Debt, in accordance with the requirements of the Government, and thus give an absolutely safe Secur- ity, the number of accounts should be considerably larger. It is to be hoped that, specially in the case of children and our growing youthful population, the facilities for small savings afforded by the Bank may induce an increase in their number and thus encourage by the saving of small sums the main object aimed at by savings banks, i.e.' the development of habits of thrift and providence. The Chairman moved the adoption of the report and the re-app)intment of the auditors Messrs. W. William- son,J. H, Salkeld, C. Pownall and W. B. Kay. He said I that he always regretted that the Chairman's speech , followed the report instead of preceding it, because ' , the report contained all that need be said about the bank's business during the past year. It was always ex- haustively drawn up and told exactly how the affairs of the bank stood, leaving nothing for the Chairman to say They all would endorse the last sentence of the report where the hope was expressed that the children would take advantage of the facilities offered •by the bank. The most pramising feature of the bank work wee that it inculcated habits of thrift in the youth of the district. When people got into old age, habits were formed and ninny of them had got into thriftless' ways. It was then too late to form new habits of thrift. But when ' they started with the youthful mind they started with a clean sheet and there was some hope in talking to chil- dren whei words might have a beneficial influence. He did not think any work done at the present time in the encouragement of thrift, could be compared with that done while the children were in school. It was a matter for congratulation that in practically every elementary school in the town a bank was formed where they could deposit their weekly savings. The misfor- tune was that after they had left school many of the children did not continue in thrifty habits and it was then that a bank—such as the savings bank—came in exceedingly useful, with the advice such as Mr. Handley incorporated with his report. Many childrenafter leaving school discontinued their thrifty habits and the Savings Bank was really instituted to take the place of the school bank. If children could be induced after leaving school to open an account in the bank there was no doubt but it would benefit them, make them better citizens and render them grateful to the bank the whole of their lives. He thanked the auditors for their labours, and Mr. Handley, actuary, for the manner in which he had conducted the affairs of the bank. He considered the report was highly satis- factory. It showed there was an increase in the num- ber of transactions, that the bank had made progress and was in a stronger financial position than before. If every depositor asked for the return of his money— which was highly improbable—the bank would have a surplus of some £2OO to dispose of. He moved the adoption of the report. Mr. Fisher seconded.

Mr. 3. Pritchard supported, remacking that it was one of the oldest useful institution S in the town, this being the bank's diamond jubilee. Mr. Hankimon also spoke.

The report wasunanimously adopted. The. Actuary said that acting on the advice of the Inspector of Savings Banks, the Bank had discontinued charging for the pass books, which involved a slight additional expenditure.

PURCHASE OF A SAFE.

The Chairman, Mr. J. M. Frith, and the Actuary were instructed to procure a safe for the Bank. A vote of thanks to the Chairman concluded the meet- ing.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS. Extensions at the Workhouse.

A meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardians was held at the Workhouse on Wednesday morning. Dr. Patterson presided and there were also present: Mrs. Clay, Rev. T. R. Matthews, Rev. Father Wilson, Dr, Murray, Messrs. A. J. Jolley, S. Davies, J. Payton, T. H. Ashcroft, J. Backhouse, J. Robinson' J. Turton, William Houghton, G. Pilling, A. Brooks, G. Mather, R.Forshaw, A. Jolley, J. Ball, T.Ritchie, E. L. Bent, J. C. Sutton, (clerk), H. Sutton (assistant clerk), F. Gate (master) William Owen (architect), W. Antrobus (assistant at the schools). THE INMATES BEHAVED THEMSELVES.

Mr. GATE reported that the usual treat to the in- mates was given on Christmas Day, aid he was pleased to report that the inmates behaved themselves in a very satisfactory manner. (Hear, hear.) The SUPERINTENDECT of the Padgate Schools also re- ported upon the treat in that institution. The children, ha said, spent a very pleasant time.

PRGI3ATIONER NURSE

A letter from the Local Government Board was read approving of the appointment of Miss Annie Read as probationer nurse._

OUTDOOR RELIEF REGULATION ORDER.

The CLERK read a letter he had received from the Clerk to the Preston Union with reference to the "Out- door Relief Regulation Order." The Clerk intimated that he had replied to the effect that the Orderhad not been in force in the Warrington Union for over 22 years.

EMPLOYMENT WANTED DURING PERIODS OF

BAD TRADE. LETTER FROM THE TRADES AND LABOUR COUNCIL.

The CLERK read a. letter from the Secretary of the Warrington Trades and Labour Coi.'ncil forwarding a copy of the resolution which had been passed by the Council, namely :—" Thlt the Secretary write to the Board of Guardians calling upon them to make pros sion to obviate the hardship entailed upon the working cliwses during the periods of had trade.' The CLERK intimated that he had repliedto the lett,er to the effect that the powers of the:Board were limited and that it was not desirable that the" Outdoor Relief Regulaaon Order,' should be put in force in the Union unless it was found to be absolutely necessary, and ad- vising the Trades and Labour Coancil to refer to the local governing bodies of the parishes of the Union to see if work could be found on reasonable payment of wages during the want of work. He added that in in- dividual cases application must be made-to the relieving officer of the district.

Mr. E. L. BENT: They want the full price for their labour.

Mr. PILLING : I think we have done quite sufficient. The CHAIRMAN: We can leave the matter in the hands of the relieving officers. The subject then dropped. THE TREATMENT OF SANE EPILEPTICS. REPORT BY MR. A. J. JOLLEY.

Mr. A. J. JOLLEY presented the following report with respect to the meeting attended by him upon thesubject of the provision of a hospital for sane epileptics :—"On December sth, 1902, I attended the adiourned meeting of the committee at the library of the Society of Chart- ered accountants, Manchester. A scheme for providing a colony in the Ribble Valley for the treatment of pauper sane epileptics chargeable to the various Lanca- shire Unions had been drawn up by Messrs. Ruddle and Bygrave' of Blackburn, and printed copies of it had been supplied to the representatives. No further explanation was deemed necessary, and the Chairman asked for expres- sions of opilion upon it. The representatives of the Gars- tang Union said that this Board would not enter into the proposed combination of unions, though he was very desirous that something should be done to improve the lot of the pauper epileptics. I pointed out that in one respect the proposals of the scheme were impraotible in view of the term of the Poor Law Act, Of 1879, and that if a combination of uaions were to be formed some.basis of apportionment of the initial cost would have to be found, and that there was here a serious, though not an insurmountable, difficulty, I further urged that Mr. Ruddle's scheme was too costly both in respect of capital and of mainteaance charges, a view which was guardedly supported by Dr. Rhodes, whose intimate knowledge of the subject as awhole and of the pra7,lical measures now being taken by the Chorlton and Man- chester Guardians for the treatment of the feeble minded poor, were of great service to the committee. • The difficulty in respect of initial cost was flu her empha- sized by Mr. Jenner Pest and Mr. Leach, of Rochdale. it was et ;dent that neither Mr. Bygrave nor Mr. Ruddle had fully realised the legal and practical difficulties in the way of providing the necessary capital for estab- lishing such a colony as had been proposed. It was at length agreed to appoint a committee of six to further consider the whole question, with a view to ascerta;n- ing whether it would be possible to combine with Manchester and Choflton or to provide a less costl3 institution than had been proposed by Mr. Ruddle." On the motion of Mr. MATHER, seconded by Mr. BENT, a vote of thanks was passed to Mr. Jolley for his repot t.

THE NEWTON RELIEF OFFICES.

A report was presented by the Committee appointed to consider the terms of occupation by Mr. Bowes of the occupation of the dwelligg-house at the new relief offices at Earlestown. After inspecting the premises occupied as a dwellitig-house by Mr. Bowes,as well as the office generally, the Committee proceeded to con- sider the sum which might equitably be debited against, him for rent of house, share of office rent for registra- tion purposes, rates on hoase and share of costs of fuel and lighting and water; and after crediting him with the probable cost of cleaning the premises, are of opinion that a payment by Mr. Bowes at the rate of £26 per annum would be equitable. Mr. BOWES wrote that upon examination. he found that itthe recommendation of the committee were con- firmed,he should be at a loss of £l6 annually, or in other Words £1.6 worse oft than he was heretofore. He couid not believe that the Board wished to saddle him with this expense every year. He had been led to understand that any suggestion of his being worse off financially was beyond the pale of serious consideration. His one desire in the matter was to be no worse off than heretofore, and if he were allowed to express an opinion he should say that this he honestly had a right to expect. The CHAIRMAN moved that the recommendation be approved. Mr. A. .T. JOLLEY seconded, and gave the detailed figures which led to the committee's recommendction. Mr. ASHCROFT thought the Board had made a mis- take in building a dwelling house in connection with the offices. Mr. Bowes had better accommodation at the new premises and he thought the recommendation ,1 of the committee a reasonable one. The Rev. T. H. MATTHEWS said he had no wish to ' question the figures they had listened to,but he thought it was tmfair for the Board to reduce Mr. Bowes in- come by so much as he had stated. There was no legal necessity why Mr. Bowes should occupy the house at the offices. His former house suited him and it was unfair for the Board to mulct him in a sum of £26 per year. Mr. FORSHAW asked whether £26 per year would clear Mr. Bowes of rent, light, fuel and water?

The CHAIRMAN: Yes. - The Rev. J. R. MATTHNWS It did not cost him that much before. .

Mr. E. L. BET: I think he has got a very cheap house, Mr. BROOKS supported the recommendation of the committee, and pointed out that a small house Was not a fit place for such an important officer as the relieving officer and registrar. The Board had hitherto tolerated Mr. Bowes doing business for them under circumstances that were inadequate. • Mr. RITCHIE thought the Board had an excellent officer in Mr. Bowes and he believed they were over- charging him in fixing rent &.c at £26 per annum. On a vote being taken the committee's recommenda- tion was approved, the Rev. T. R, Matthews and 'AI r. Ritchie voting against it.

INFLUENZA INCREASING.

The Influenza Epidemic is again spreading over the Country. The visitation is of a very prostrating nature, and the disease is certainly on the increase. The origm of the disease or rather the circumstances which bring it about is as much a mystery as ever. You cannot trifle with Influenza. It undermines the Nervous System to a greater extent than almost any other disease, and it is Pneumonia that kills, and Pneumonia strikes the man who imagines he has only a mild form of Influenza, and can go about the street just asusual. It can hardly •be doubted that the prevalence of Chest and Lung Complaints must be ascribed to the baneful influence of Influenza, whose after-effects so frequently take this form. It was a bad day for this Country when this malignant scourge first visited our shores. Year after year it has never since failed to revive at its due time, and to extort its tribute of human life. Having observed its ravages, and its dreadful effects upon the constitution on the occasion of its former visits, we cannot but regard its recurrence with much fear, as a great many have not yet, if they ever will,regain their tormar health. As a preventative, we know of nothing equal to Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters. A few strong doses taken in time have often provedeffectual in ward- ing (Al attacks of this Malady, and alwar succeed in mitigating their severity when a patient is under their influence. Do you suffer from Chills, alternating why Sudden Flushes, Severe Head-ache, accompanied bh difficulty of breathing, and symptoms or Catarrh, Pains in the Chest, under and between the shoulders, and a stiffness and soreness of the Muscles, Loss of Appetite and Nervousness. If you suffer from any of these symp- toms, know that they are the forerunners of Influenza, audit behoves you to resort at once to a course of Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters. By commen consent Patients overywhere admit that they feel more depressed and miser- able after an attack of Influenza than while under its influence, and more than they feel after an attack of any other illness. The best way to banish these miser- able feelings is to take Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters, The Vegetable Tonic. We can strongly and confidently recommend this preparation, as it has treated severe cases of Influenza. Heavy Colds. Extreme Weakness, and Langour, with greater success than any other known Remedy. We may also add that as a Remedy for Indi- gestion, Sleeplessness, Low Spirits, Nervousness. and Loss of Appetite, Gwilym Evans' Quinine Bitters stands unequalled. It is sold in bottles 2s. 9d. and 4s. 6d., by all Chemists and Patent Medicine Vendors, or sent direct, carriage free, for the above prices, by the sole proprietors: Quinine Bitters Manufacturing Company Limited Llanelly, South Wales. BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES

Minor Cases.

Warrington Borough Court.

SATURDAY.—Before Mr. Birchall (in the chair) Dr. Fox and Messrs Hayward and R. T. Fairclough. COWARDLY CONDUCT.--JaMeS Doherty, Bank-street was charged with being drunk and disorderly in Bank- street on the previous evening and assaulting Elizabeth Ryan, Dolman's-lane.—From the evidence it appeared that the prisoner, who was very drunk and made use of disgusting language, kicked several children who had come out of the Ragged School. Mrs. Ryan remonstra- ted with him and he kicked her. P.S. Evans and P.C. Houghton came upon the scene, and prisoner was taken into custody. Two of the children who had been hurt were taken to the Infirmary, but Dr. Mort said they were not badly injured.—Prisoner was fined 10s. and nosts, the Chairman remarking that this "kicking busi- cess "was getting very common.

MONDAY.—Before Mr. F. Monks (in the chair), Alder- man Smethurst, Messrs. Birchall, Wright. Hayward,

and Thornton.

A PRISONER'S VlOLlCE.—William Mulligan, 8, Silver-street, was sentenced to a month's imprisonment for having been drunk and disorderly in Winwick-road at 3.40 on Saturday afternoon and assaulting P.C.Cooper (20).—The officer was assisting two men to take home another man who was helplessly drunk, when the prisoner interfered and made use of filthy language. He was taken into custody and commenced to be very vio- lent, kicking the constable severely. The assistance of P.C. Hayes was obtained and on the way to the lock-up, , prisoner struck P.C. Cooper in the face.—Prisoner made his fourth appearance before the magistrates during the year. MA PAUPER'S ESCAPADE—Peter Caldwell, an inmate of the Workhouse, was charged with absenting himself from that institution on Saturday and not performing the task allotted to him.—Mr. Gate, the Workhouse Master, said that on Saturday the prisoner, instead of being at work, was in town, and witness met him near the Infirmary, in a drunken condition. He was then wearing clothing which did not belong to the Board. He had evidently got over the Workhouse wall, *one to some friend's and changed his clothing in order to have a drunken" fuddle "without being recognised by the police, and afterwards put on the Workhouse clothing and returned to the House.—Prisoner made his third appearance.—The Chairman said they would send him where he would be made to work. He would be committed for a month with hard labour.

FIGHTLNG.—Patrick Moran, 15, Portland.street, and Michael Scully, 9, Dakin's-yard, were summoned for fighting on Pig Hill on December 23rd.—Moran admitted that it was his fault that the fight occurred.—Defend- ants were bound over to keep the peace, Scully in his own surety only, and Moran to find another surety in addition to his own. _ _

_ No LIGHTS.—John Hatton, butcher, Lymm, was fined 5s for not having a lighted lamp attached to a vehicle which he was driving on the night of December 22nd. A number of witnesses were called, defendant stating that the lamp was lighted when he left the lVfarket Place, and when P.C. Hamblett called his attention to the fact that it had gone out at Warrington Bridge, it was then warm.—The officer stated that defendant drove on some distance before pulling up.—Patrick McCoy and John Thompson, carters, were fined, the former 5s and costs, and the latter 2s 6d for similar offences.— McCoy did not appear. CHIMNEY ON FIRE.—A. B. Garner and Company, Sankey-street,were fined 3s 6d for allowing the chimney of their shop to be on fire.

Daresbury Petty Sessions.

TUESDAY.—Befure Messrs. W. Long (in the chair), F. Monks, H. Nield, Roger Parr, and Joseph Knowles. HORSES ILLTREATED.—George Buckley, Knutaford- road, Warrington, was summoned for having on the 12th December, at Walton, illtreated a horse by causing it to be worked whilst in an unfit state on the 12th December, and John Rutter, Hardy-street, Warrington, was charged with working the animal. P.C. Miller .fdeposed to meeting a horse and cart at Walton. The citt was filled with sand. The animal was lame and in palii.• Inspector Print (R.S.P.C.A.), said the animal was suffering from ringbone and sidebone. It was quite , unfit for work. Buckley was fined 10s. and costs and Rutter in the costs.—Thomas Ditchlield,of Grappenhall (who did not appear), was charged with a similar offence at Latchford on the 15th December. P.C. Williams said the animal, which was in an emaciated condition, had .since been destroyed. Inspector Print said the animal was completely worn out. As it had since been des- troyed the Bench decided to dismiss the case.—Peter Ackers, Cookson-street, Liverpool, was summoned for a similar offence at Daresbury on the 6th December. He did not appear, and was fined 10s. and 00StS. BREVITIES.—The following minor cases were dealt with :—Mary Howard, Queen-str.let, Warrington, for acting as a pedlar without a licence was fined in the costs; Fred Hughes, Runcorn, riding a bicycle on the footpath at Walton, to pay the costs; Joseph Dutton Australia-lane, Grappenhall, using profane language a. Grappenhall, fined 55.; Percy TUson, Higher 'Walton, riding a bicycle at Grappenhall on the 6th December at 5.10 p.m. without a light, fined 3s. 6d.; John Connell, Nicholson-street, Warrington, driving a cab at one a.m. .st Stockton Heath,without a lighted lamp attached to it, fined 55.; Frank Hamblett, driving a horse and dray at 5.40 p.m. without a light at Walton, fined ss; Samuel Done and Maud Sununers, allowing dogs to be at large without muzzle or collar, fined 3s. tid. each. IN THE WRONG COURT.—George Simcock was charged with having, on the 27th December, assaulted a neigh- bour named Andrew Kelvin at Grappenhall.—The com- plainant said he was building a wooden shed in his garden at Grappenhall at 1.30 p.m. on the day in ques- tion, when the defendant rushed through the door and said "I am going to pull this shed down." The defend- ant then—The Clerk (Mr. Henry Greenall, to the defendant): Are you going to contend that this is your land ?—Defendant ! Yes, sir.—The Clerk (to the com- plainant) : Then the case will have to go to the County Court, as the magistrates have no jurisdiction.— Defendant: But it is a question of assaulting me.—The Clerk: Not at all.—Defendant: But he had no right to assault me.—The Clerk: You can recover damages; if you have been assaulted you can get damages.—The case was dismissed.

uding costs.—lt was stated that in a drunken freak he bad smashed fifteen panes of glass. EATING CHIP POTATOES AFTER ELEVEN O'CLOCK.— Thomas Ashcroft, High-street, was summoned for keep- ing his refreshment house open after the hour of 11 p.m. on the 20th December.—Superintendent Ennion said that on the date mentioned he and P.C. Edwards after 11 o'clock saw a light in the defendant's chip potatoe shop, in the theatre whit, and on obtaining admittance found eight men eating chips in the shop. The defendant said to the Superintendent he did not want his chips to be wasted fter they had been cooked.—John Donnellan excise officer proved that Ashcroft held a licence authorising him to close his house at.ll p.m.—Ashcroft, who did not appear was fined £1 including costs or in default fourteen days. A BAREFACED THEFT.-At the RWICOM Police Court on Saturday before Messrs. W. Timmins (in the chair) and Samuel Taylor, a young married woman named Sarah Hughes was summoned for wilfully stealing a suit of clothes valued at £2 2s. belonging to Ellen Morris on Saturday.—The evidence showed that the prisoner went to Samuel Dodds, pawnbroker, on the date in question and after transacting her business picked up a suit of clothes belonging to Mrs. Morris, just redeemed. The prisoner afterwards pledged the coat at Edwin Imison's,pawnbroker, returning to Dodds with the trousers and vest, where she was arrested by P.C. Davies.—Prisoner swore she picked up the suit by mistake.—The bench said the theft was a serious one.— She would be fined £1 or in default fourteen days.

MAGAZINE CUTTINGS.

Impressions of England.

No country has been more sympathetic to me than England, and in spite of the coldness of which the English are accused I have no cause to complain of any want of warmth in my receptions. The English too are faithful in their attachments, and once they discover power in an artist they are true to their admiration for this power, and speaking from my own experience,Eng- land always seems with regard to me to exhibit the perfect entente eordtale between the French and English which the Society of that name strives to express, and at whose soiree I assisted in London. Nothing is more touching than the proud and ardent affection of the English for Shakespeare. . . "Shakespeare is often' felt to be the invisible and latent link between us and other lands," said a well-known, Englishman to me one day, and I think that this superior man was right. It is sad, very sad to have to say that the Latin races are those who have the least love for the theatre, and it may be due to the indisputable fact that these same races are struggling against decadence.—From "The Moral Influence of the Theatre," by Madame Bernhardt in the" Cornhill Magazine."

===== 0002601_19030102_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
Destroyers of Destroyers.

The "scout': is an entirely new class of warship. The Admiralty decided not to initiate a design, but to in- vite the private ship-builders to give the Navy the benefit of their creative ingenuity by submitting de- signs to fulfil certain stated conditions. The four "scouts" provided for,in the Navy Estimates of the current financial year are to be built by the Fairfield Company, Messrs. Armstrong, Whitworth and Co., Messrs. Laird Brothers, and Messrs. Vickers, Sons, and Maxim. We gather that they are to be vessels of 3,000 tons displacement, of 25 knots speed, and are to possess good sea-going qualities. Such small ships as these should find useful spheres of action in a Naval engage- ment. They will present but a small target to the enemy's gunners, and both for scouting purposes and for chasing torpedo-boat destroyers should prove their value. They have been called "destroyers, of des- troyers," and a vessel of this class was bound to come sooner or later. The next step will be to design a destroyer of destroyers of destroyers. The nine new torpedo-boat destroyers now in building are to be of the 25i-knot type and of much stronger construction than the 30-knot type. Mr. Yarrow and Messrs. Thornycroft have built 30-knot destoyers which have steamed un- aided to Japan in all conditions of weather, and it is to be regretted that the Admiralty should have, for the present at all events, given up the swifter type and none in for the slower and surer craft.—" Navy and Army.'

The Dalai Lama in 1882.

This Being at the time of his visit was about nine years old, much the same age as the little Incarnate Buddhas seen by Manning and Hue on their respective visits. The hall at the top of the palace in which the poor little fellow sat was full of soletnn lamas, motion- less and silent as the grave, each with his eyes fixed steadily on the tip of his own nose. In the midst of this grave assemblage sat the sacred head of the Budd- hist religion, a bright, fa r-complexioned boy with rosy cheeks, large and penetrating eyes. and an Aryan type of oountenance. His frame was thin with fastings and prayers, and one cannot hell) feeling heartsick at the thought of the poor child, a mere puppet in reality though invested with so much saactity, cut off by no fault of his own from all the joys of youth, and prob- ably destined to die a violent death in his early man- hood, since the powers that be,prefer a young andhelp- less Dalai Lama. No wonder that Mending, when he had visited the Dalai Lama of his time, could think of nothing but the beautiful face of the doomed child, and that he felt his eyes full of tears.--From " In the Heart of the Forbidden Country; or, Lhasa Revealed," in the Cornhill Magazine" for January. President Castro.

One day last week I was fortunate enough to meet a personal friend of President Castro who has just returned from Venezuela. He tells me that the President is physically a very small man, of an excitable tempera- ment, but well educated, and has the eloquence of Demosthenes. From the first time Castro addressed the Chambers of Deputies his name has been asuociated with the Presidency. Apart from international inter- ests the relations between the English Consul—Mr. Haggard (who is a brother of Rider Haggard)—and the President have been for a long time or a very strained personal nature. But this is not a latter-day feature of Venezuelan diplomacy; as far back as 1881 the President and the British Consul publicly insulted one another in the streets of Caracas. I understand that President Castro also feelsaggrieved because the day on which Mr. Haggard handed the ultimatum to him was a great feast day in Venezuela. and thp President was called away from entertaining some friends in order to receive the papers."—" The King." •

The Viceregal Rule of India.

"Lord Curzon belongs to, and brings op to present date, the line of a most remarkable dynasty of rulers,al- - all of them capable men, and some of eminent ad- ministrative ability," says Sir Edwin Arnold in a valuable article whichappears in the January "Windsor Magazine." True, the political history of the British in India begins no further back than 1629 A.D., when Thomas Day founded Fort St. George,at; Madras. We were so humble then that we paid a yearly tribute of twelve hundred pagodas to the Mogul Sultan, Aurangzeb, and surrendered Madras to a French squadron almost with- out a blow, Dupleix at that time being Governor of Poudichem. But, in truth—as I have elsewhere written—if we will trace the special river of .British Indian history to its source, it may be demonstrated that the grandeur which Lord Curzon went out to in- herit and assume,derives itself entirely from a doctor's Iprescription. In 1636 A.D. the daughter-in-law of Shah Jehan, and favourite wife of Sultan Shuja, Nawab of Bengal, who was the second son of the Great Mogul, lay sick of a malady beyond the skill of all his Mohammedan hakims. Distressed at the danger of one so fair and precious, the Nawab ealled to his aid the surgeon of the East India Company's ship Hopewell, by name Gabriel Broughton, a clever young doctor, who, although not allowed to see the beautiful face pf his royal patient, all the same effected a perfect cure. The grateful Prince asked him to name his own fee, whereupon Broughton begged for,and obtained,a firman granting permission to the East India Company to trade throughout the dominions of the great Moguls, and giving them land to build a factory, which factory has since grown up to become the stately city of Calcutta. But Clive, the young 'writer,' was awaiting his hour in India, and with his energy and indisputable genius the strange story of England in India definitely commenced. The victories of Arcot and Plessey started and estab- lished the orderly succession of Governor-Generals, as they were then called,'and Plessey, be it remembered, was not fought until 1757. In 1758,Clive was nominated first Governor of all the East India Company's settle- ments in Bengal. And after him' a line of some five- and-twenty vice-rulers has carried the British Raj through its many vicissitudes of glory and of peril."— From the January "Windsor Magazine.

The Examiner, Friday, January 2, 1903. AT HOME AND ABROAD.

The Costly Automobile.

It is interesting to consider the wealth of resource which goes to the making of an automobile. Nearly all the metals are required—silver for the lamp reflectors, aluminium for the body and engine, platinum for the spark points, zinc and carbon for the battery, lead for packing, tin for tubes,and copper, iron, several kinds of steel, brass and bronze for the machinery and fittings. Even gold is employed—and that in large quantities— for the purchase of the machine. Oriental potentates use this also for handle-bars and fittings, and in a few cases, costly jewels also have been requisitioned. Other necessaries are leather, asbestos, wood, glass, paper, canvas, rubber, porcelain, linen, wool and hair. And to assemble all these commodities only the most skilled workmanship will serve.—" Motoring Illustrated." The Waste of Public Money. -In the second of his trenchant articles on this all- important theme in the January "Windsor Magazine," Mr. Ernest Williams sums up :—" I have dealt at length with this Remount question because it is symptomatic and illustrative. It was all very well to adopt the line taken by Lord Morley in defending the Government in the House of Lords, when he asked: "Was it fair to assume that the revelations as to the remounts,were only lifting up a comer of the curtain which concealed the utter want of business-like administrative capacity on the part of the Government ?" and to assert that an assumption' 'was generalising in a very general and hasty way.' This will not be the public view. It is difficult to get at the facts in many matters pertaining to Government work. Ministers and officials themselves- take care of that with their policy of secrecy. But they must court publicity, and not struggle against it,if they wish the pubilc to believe that such scandals as have been discovered are the only scandals that exist. The Remount scandals do not stand alone, even among the instances of waste and lack of business which the recent war has brought to light. At the very time the Government apologists were trying to get the public to believe that the Re- mount scandal did stand alone, the facts concerning the Meat Contracts were being dragged into light—and how scandalous they were I have already related. And though I cannot hope to make a complete record of all the waste of public money which is being perpetrated, I have yet some more instances to bring to your notice. They will form cumulative and presumptive proof of 'the utter want of business-like administrative capacity on the part of the Government.' It ' br itended that these administrative

The Govern- utmost to do him damage. ment, however, with its huge majority, It was in February that General Delarey carried the bill through, and it is now upon routed a body of our Infantry and Yeomanry the Statute-Book. and took Lord Methuen prisoner after he had been severely wounded. Reluctantly the Boers came to appreciate they were hopelessly out- numbered, and, although hot skirmishing continued, their ardour for fighting waned, and talk began as to terms of peace. Lord Kitchener gave special permission for Steyn, Botha, Delarey, De Wet, and Schalk Burger to meet at Klerksdorp. Afterwards they visited Pretoria, had conferences with Lord Kitchener, and they visited their various commandos to gain the general view as to surrender. Later on they met at Vereeniging, the result of which was that at half-past ten on Saturday night, May 31st, peace was signed on terms which amounted practically to unconditional surrender, although the British undertook to provide large sums for the reinstating of farmers in their homesteads. It was on the following day news reached England that peace was declared, and great was the excitement and jubilation all over the country. Church bells rang, crowds gathered in the streets and hurrahed, and for several days everybody owning a piece of bunting hung it from his window. In the middle of July Lord Kitchener came home. I witnessed his arrival in London, and nothing could have exceeded the outburst of enthusiasm with which this long, lanky, cold-mannered General was received. Later on we had the three famous Boer Generals—Botha, Delarey, and De Wet—with us, and the reception accorded them by the British public was one that might have been mistaken for the greeting of successful warriors instead of erstwhile enemies who had been subjected to defeat. One of the "blessings in disguise" of the Boer War was that it knit together all sections of the British Empire. A little coldness had grown up between the Mother Country and her Colonies, -but in the hour of distress the Colonies loyally jumped to our support, and no men fought more bravely for us than our kinsmen from beyond seas. Mr. Chamberlain took advantage of this by calling a conference in London of the Colonial Premiers who were visiting this country to attend the Coronation. Not much was the outcome except a very frank exchange of views, and the paving of the way before long of knitting together in closer bonds of political and commercial relationship Great Britain and her Colonies. Further, Mr. Chamberlain decided to proceed to South Africa to consider on the spot the best means of establishing a satisfactory Government in that country, and to provide for its com- mercial development. This month closes with a combined naval demonstration on the part of Greta Britain and Germany before various Venezuelan ports. For some years • British and German subjects who have had commercial transactions with Venezuela have not onlay been unable to obtain payment, but there has been a disposi- tion on the part of the Venezuelan Government to repudiate liability. Both England and Germany exhausted the resources of diplomacy in trying to obtain satisfaction, and the threat to seize harbours and customs until the money was obtained was regarded as an empty threat by the Venezuelans, because they expected that the Govemment of the United States would prevent any warlike operations in American. waters. The Washington Government, how- ever, recognised the legitimacy of the demand made by the Foreign Officesin London and Berlin, and have stood on one side without helping their brother Americans in the South.

THE POLITICS OF THE WORLD.

The principal thing as regards Great Britain in what is called "high politics" has been the alliance we have made with Japan. This is the first occasion when a great Western Power has entered into such a treaty with an Eastern nation. The announcement of the alliance was greeted with acclamation, not only because British people have a sentimental affection for the Japanese, but because the enormous stride that Japan has been making this last quarter of a century to put aside Eastern ways, so that in all current things it may rank shoulder to shoulder with Western ideas, has been appreciated, and also because we have dual interests in that part of the world not likely to conflict, and therefore the .joining of hands would probably reduce the possibility of an outbreak of war. This was the popular interpretation, though many serious students of Eastern politics have thrown grave doubts on the wisdom of the action of our Government. The Japanese are on the search for new territory which can be made into colonies to accommodate their over- crowded population, for since the Chino- Japanese War our Japanese friends have had enormous ideas of their military and naval prowess, and are, generally speaking, itching for a war with Russia, which they regard as their natural enemy. Students of the East rather fear that willy-nilly the Japanese may draw us into a conflict with Russia respecting Corea—a country with which we have com- paratively little direct interest, but one towards which both Russia and Japan cast envious P.

Russia, in her quiet manner, continues her acquisition of fresh territory. As everybody knew whose business was not in Downing- street, the much-talked-of " evacuation " of Manchuria by the Russians was never any- thing else but politically Piekwickian. Russia is in Manchuria. She is there to stay, and it is nobody's business to turn her out. Indeed, it is currently understood in diplo- matic circles that there exists a secret treaty between Russia and ourselves, that Great Britain will not interfere with the great Muscovite Power in Manchuria so long as we are given a fairly free hand in the rich and fertile Yangtze Valley. The point of conflict which we have with Russia is in regard to Persia. British influence in the north of the land of Iran has undoubtedly waned, whilst that of Russia has prospered. The Russians are keenly anxious to have a port in the Persian Gulf. Indeed, I might say they are determined on getting it. Only the declaration of war would prevent this, unless—which is far more likely, for neither oountry is desirous of plunging into a conflict —some other arrangement could be made whereby, in return for British non-intervention In allowing Russia to have a port in the Persian Gulf, Russia shall stand aside in some other matter we have in hand.

Looking the world round, there has been a marked tendency this year on the part of all great Powers to avoid the possibility of war with one another. France has cut down her Mediterranean fleet so that she would be help- less at sea in the case of an outbreak of hostilities. The bitter animosity in Germany, so rampant at the early part of the year towards ourselves, has largely disappeared. 7rermans found violent antipathy only led to the losing of British trade. Besides, we, have very common interests in Africa. Thel recent visits of the German Emperor and the King of Portugal to these shores, though probably lacking those high diplomatic reasons which have been ascribed to them by some people, certainly did tend in one direction— Great Britain and Germany are now acting in harmony, as in Venezuela, for instance. A. Cape-to-Cairo railway, running through terri- tory belonging to both Powers, will be of advantage to both. And the sale of Delagoa Bay to Britain by the Portuguese will not only give us a valuable stretch of the South African continent, but will replenish the scant *offers of the Portuguese Government.

HOME POLITICS FOR THE YEAR. The closing year will stand out as one of marked interest in politics. Lord Salisbury resigned the high place of Prime Minister, and the King called upon Mr. Balfour to take the office. Since the passing away of Mr. Gladstone Lord Salisbury has certainly been the most dignified figure in the political arena. Now that he no more concerns himself with active politics, but is spending his declining days experimenting :soi his laboratory at Hatfield—for it has always been known the Marquess has taken a much more personal interest in scientific matters than political—a commanding figure has disappeared from the House of Peers. With him gone, there practically only remains the Earl of Rosebery among their lordships as a man standing ' shoulder-high above his fellows in interest to the public. The Gilded Chamber seems the poorer for the absence of the Marquess. Tall, broad, commanding, with a scholarly though somewhat heavy countenance, Lord Salisbury's intervention in debates always raised them above humdrum commonplace, into which they were too apt to slide. There was a lofti- ness of mind, a ripeness of phrase, in his speeches which placed them in a far higher alti- tude than any others delivered for a long time in the Lords. Lord Rosebery is a more popular speaker, more dramatic, more directly m touch with the sympathies of the "man in the street," more sparkling, witty, and illuminative than his great antagonist who, until the middle of July, sat on the other side of the table. There was however, a certain majesty in Lord Salisbury's manner which always made him the more deeply interesting man. Lord Cadogan has ceased to be Lord- Lieutenant of Ireland, and although there was some expectation that the young Duke of Marl- porough would be appointed in his stead, it was the handsome Earl of Dudley to whom the high position ultimately fell. Lord Dudley has in a few months made himself most agreeable to the Irish people, not only by showing the liveliest interest in their welfare, but by taking an unofficial residence, and by making frequent tours throughout the little green isle. He has been assisted largely by his captivating wife, who was not long before she endeared herself to the Hibernians. Her recent severe illness, which for a short time took her to the doors of death, not only aroused sympathy, but demonstrated how SON she had gained possession of the affections E. the people. IN THE SILENCE OF THE NIGHT. 1 Collier's Awful Visitation.

Much interest has been aroused (says the "Wrexham Advertiser ") by the thrilling story of an experience such as rarely fitlls to the lot of colliers, often as the work brings them into peril. Inquiries show the affair I to be even stranger and of more public interest than I reported. Mr. David Evans is well known in St. Martins Moors, Chirk, having lived there many years. The facts were fully stated by his wife. "On February 21 last, Mrs. .Evans began, "my hus- band went to work attlack Park Colliery, as usual. He returned from work apparently quite well, and re-: tired to bed at the usual hour. Next morning, however, my husband could not get 'out o fbed, having had a stroke of paralysis during the night. It seized him all ' down one side,and he was unable to move. In spite of the doctor's skill he had no hope of recovery. At this moment of despair we tried Dr. Williams' pink pills for pale people, which completely restored my husband to health." "What was it induced you to get them ?" "I had seen them spoken of in the papers, and I said 'We will try them, for if they don't cure they can't ; harm.' We bought the pills in Oswestry, and from ; lying on his back in bed, helpless and almost speech- less, I less, n four or five weeks he was able to get out of bed. Remember that up till then he had been completely paralysed on one side—as helpless as a child, and unable to do the least thing for himself. We had eleven boxes of the pills, but before he had taken them all, my husband was quite well again and able to go to work." ; No more awful affliction than paralysis exists. The ' early signs of it are shaky hands, especially in the morn- ing. loss of sensation in the limbs, coldness at the extremities, spinal weakness, uncertainty of walk, and loss of virility. If any of these symptoms are neglected, a stroke may come, and the sufferer will be crippled and In agony. Dr. Williams' pink pills have cured the worst forms of paralysis, including locomotor ataxy; their powerful bracing aotion on the spine makes them a valuable preventive. They give strength and make blood, curing anemia and consumption, and the ail- ments of both sexes rheumatism, kidney disease, indi- gestion, weakness and premature age. It is a terrible and ghastly mistake to accept substitutes in place of the genuine medicine, forsubstitutes never cured any- one, and the dread disease may make rapid strides,when the genuine pills would save the sufferer. Therefore, see ths full name, Dr. Williams' pink pills, on the wrapper. It is better to prevent than cure. Let no one risk such a terrible visitation as that which attacked the Wrexham collier in the dread silence of the night.

WARRINGTON.

Personal Mr. Bertram S. Chandley has accepted the position of engineer and manager to the St. Mary Cray Gas Company, Kent. Included in the list just published of the results of the Incorporated Accountants examinations held this month is the name of Mr: John Whiteman Welsby, of Warrington, who passed the preliminary examination. Sudden Deaths. The police have been informed of the sudden deaths of William Bate aged 52 who resided in General stree4 and Mrs. Marsh, wife of Mr. A. B. Marsh, insurance agent, 3 Rolleston-street, which took place on Monday. In each case death is supposed to have been due to heart disease.

Daresbury Sessions. At Tuesday's Daresbury Petty Sessions, the chair- man (Mr. W. Long) annonnced that owing to a change in the licensing laws, the old and tune-honoured custom of meeting._ there on the last Tuesdtly in the month was altered from that day, and the Bench would meet in future on the second Tuesday in the month.

Young Men's Dance. On Boxing night a dance promoted by the Young Men's Association attached to St. Elphin's Church, was held and passed off very successfully. Capital arrangements had been made by a Committee. Nearly a hundred couples were present. and dancing was kept up with zest until the early hours of the morning. The music was supplied by Mr. George Lomas, the refreshments by Mrs. Lowe. The room had been tastefully decorated. The M.C.'s were Messrs. R. Antwiss and J. Hill.

A Tribute to the Late Primate. The Rev. A. S. Cameron paid a tribute to the mem ory of the late Primate in the course of his sermon a- the Parish Church on Sunday. Preaching on "The gifts we should give to Christ," he said their thoughts turned naturally to that strenuous and indefatigab e worker, the great Archbishop Who had been so lately called away--a man who took up the greatest task the Church of England has to offer, at an age when most men's work in life was done. It had been said that if irrespective of ecclesiastical position the Eng- lish people could have chosen the man best fitted to place the crown on the head of Edward VII., Frederick Temple would have been chosen by acclaim. Earnestly might they thank God for such a noble ex- ample.

Runcorn Choir at Chester Eisteddfod.

The fourteenth Christmas Eisteddfod was held in the Music Hall, Chester, on .Boxing Day. Out of the four entries for the Male Voice Choir Contest only two choirs appeared on the platform, viz., Rhos Choir (conductor, Mr. Wilfrid Jones, R.A.M.) .and the Run- corn Male Voice Choir (conductor, Mr. H. Berrey). The test piece was "The Britons"' (Prothero). The adjudicator, in making the award to the Rhos Choir, referred to what was noticed by the audience, namely, that the Runcorn conductor faced the audience and sang the tenor solo himself and in the excitement got out of tune. It was no uncommon thing, said Di. Coward, for a conductor to get excited, but th e con- ductor's failure, however, threw the Runcorn Choir out Of the contest. They opened very well and the ring of the basses was • exceedingly good. The pure tone and ring of the tenors also impressed him favour- ably. At the climax they did not have the same strong effect, but up to this point it was a question as to which was the better. From the solo the contest was over. The conductor got excited and lost the note. He must say, however, that the choir picked them- selves up well and sang through very good, except in one place where the basses were a little uncertain. He must award the prize to the Rhos Choir.

THE STORY or THE YEAR,

BY JOHN FOSTER FRASER.

The year 1902, now ebbing to its close, has had much sunshine and shadow in it. It has witnessed the crowning of King Edward VII. of England and King Alfonso XIII. of Spain. It has also marked the termination of a three years' struggle between the British and the Boers for domination in South Africa, a war waged with courage and determination on both sides; but now that it is all over, leaving practically no rancour behind, Boer and Briton have recognised each other's merits and are quickly settling down to harmonious working, so that before long it is likely South Africa will outrival both Canada and Australia as a great Colony of the Mother Country. On the other side, there is to record one of the most disastrous occurrences in history, namely, the overwhelming of Martinique by the eruption of Mont Pel6e, in the West Indies, which brought sudden, awful death to some 60,000 persons, and in tragedy out- rivalled the destruction of Pompeii and Herculaneum. The year closes with military operations against the Mullah in Somaliland, and with the armed intervention of Great Britain and Germany into the affairs of Venezuela, which has for several years ignored paying its debts to British and German subjects. As far as Great Britain is concerned, the year ends with anything but a bright com- mercial outlook. Once more in all the poor districts of our great cities is heard the cry of the unemployed. But, if one may hold the balance, I think no one can deny that, taking the brightness with the blackness, there has been more sunshine in the twelve months that have gone than gloom. Any way, we have been relieved from those long, direful casualty lists which, during the three preceding years, brought mourning to many a British home.

===== 0002601_19030102_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CORONATION.

Generations of British children will recite the date of 1902 as the year when King Edward VII. came to the throne. Those children of the far future will, I doubt not, regard it not over affectionately, because to their juvenile minds it will be nothing but one bead in a string of interminable dates when English kings were crowned. To us, however, of this generation—until we are grey grandfathers and while the power of story-telling is still with us—this will be a red-letter year, and we will recall stories that will delight all the little ones of the time when the seventh of the Edwards had the crown of England placed upon his head in the historic Abbey of Westminster. It was an event which not only thrilled the whole British Empire—coming as it. did at the close of the long and arduous Boer. War— but it aroused interest among all the nations of the earth. It was a great pageant. The panoply of centuries was pressed into one great religious service. People came from, all parts of the world, chiefly, of course, from our daughter colonies, which sent lithe, long- limbed young warriors to shew the sort of men made in Australia, in Canada, and New Zealand. Dusky features 'and radiant garbs from the East demonstrated Britain's place as a mighty world power. From the United States came hosts of Americans. This is a utilitarian, practical age; but it was only the unimaginative, the slow-minded who did not see in the Coronation much that was symbolic in the marriage of King Edward to nis people, besides being the occasion for military display. The old wife's story had been going the round that Edward VII, although he would come into his kingship, would never live to be crowned. It looked at one time as if this was going to be verified. Long months before it had been decided 'that the Coronation of King Edward, with the accompanying crowning of Queen Alexandra as his Consort, should take place' on Thursday, June 26th; that on the following day the King and Queen should drive in magnificent state through London; and the day after that there should be a demonstration of England's naval strength by way of a ieview at Spithead. The best-laid plans of mice and men, how- ever, "gang aft agley," and the whole pro- gramme was suddenly upset by the dramatic illness of the King, which sent a shudder of consternation through all his people. There was a military review arranged to take place at Laffan's Plain, near Aldershot, on Monday, ' June 16th. On the Saturday preceding, a damp and disagreeable evening, there was a 1 torchlight tattoo before the King. The next day we heard his Majesty was ill with a cold. On the Monday the review took place in alternate drenchings of rain and gleams of sunshine. The King, however, was not there. The Prince of Wales took his father's place, and near him was his Queen-mother and his Royal relatives. I remember how sadly worn and anxious they looked. It was thought the chill would pass, but that for safety's sake the King should, not expose himself but return to Windsor. The follow- ing week he was not able to attend Ascot Races, generally a brilliant Royal function, and intended to be especially brilliant this year, as it was on the eve of the Coronation. But again only the Prince of Wales and the Queen attended. On Monday, June 22nd, the King and Queen came from the Royal Borough to take up their residence in London, preparatory to the great ceremony of their lives. No Monarch ever received so great a welcome as the King when he drove to Buckingham Palace. London was half-smothered with decorations, and nightly the illuminations made Piccadilly, Pall Mall, and the Strand as gorgeous as the Arabian Nights' tales. All the Royal Princes from abroad were gathered at Buckingham Palace at a great State dinner. The King, however, was again nbt present. Once more it was thought he was a little overcome with his journey, and not being in good health he was resting. Tuesday came bright end beautiful. In the middle of the day—when all London was in raptures at the approaching Coronation, now only a day and a-half distant—the news came like a thunderclap that the King was danger. ously ill, and that a most grave surgical operation was being performed upon him. What a tragedy! All the Empire bubbling with enthusiasm, and the King, for whom it all was, lying insensible and under the surgeon's knife! Nothing perhaps in the history of the world ever caused such a shock as that awful news. The cheers died away, and people spoke in whispers. Mirthful countenances became mournful. The King was apparently dying, and the flags and the bunting seemed almost an insult to the people's grief. It was a ' national calamity, and every man and woman felt as interested in the King's welfare as I though he were the dearest, closest relative. We all recall the days of anxiety, of breath- less waiting, of dread and fear, whilst the 1 King battled with death. But owing to his I splendid constitution he conquered the fell I disease. It is no glib remark to say that the 1 hearts of his people were with him, and with ' his girlish-faced wife, Queen Alexandra, who was -his constant nurse. It was known that worse than the surgeon's knife and his own physical distress was the sorrow his Majesty felt that everybody had been disappointed. • Very gradually he recovered, and he fixed August 9th as the day on which the postponed Coronation should take place. Many people thought this was unwise, because he was then still an invalid, and there was more than the possibility of a breakdown. He was deter- mined, however, not to keep off the ceremony peqqoa sea uoppiooo eq4 gartoiri .1118 4911311 V 1;t0 puy Snsseoeu swat uvill Je2uoi SIT s
