THE COMMERCE OF THE WORLD.

The year closes anything but brightly in regard to trade. Germany has been suffering for several years from depression, and now there are grave indications that we ourselves are in for a stretch of slackness. Few of the great industries of Great Britain are working up to their capacity. Once more we hear the bitter cry of the unemployed and the sorely dis- -assed. The British people are proverbially -4cm-hearted, and therefore there is little *t the well-to-do will give of their plenty. matter of deep moment to all actively mterested in commercial prospecity is why! Great Britain should be struck by the wave of comnlercial depression whilst across the . seas the Americans are on the crest of prosperity. This past year, as never before, the British people have begun to realise how serious is the trade rivalry with our trans. Atlantic cousins. The too common habit of sneering at American goods and methods of production has ceased except in the minds of the obtuse, and manufacturers and workmen have realised that if we are going to hold any- thing like our own we must adapt ourselves as far as possible to American ways. I personally spent several months there this year studying the industrial position in large manufacturing centres of the United States. Various descriptions which I wrote of the agility, the alertness, the adaptability of the Americans were repudiated by slow.going British manu- facturers with a kink In their brain which prevented them understanding ihat isnybody can do things better than themselves. What I wrote was described as exaggeration. 'Ev7iry- thing, however, has been corrobocatO and amplified by the commission of representative workers sent over from this country to America by Mr. Moseley. More than a mild sensation was caused by a giant syndicate, controlled by Mr. Pierpont Morgan, buying up several British fleets of Steamers. and making it. snore flan nossible

that the old line that Britain "rules the waves" will have to be altered. There was, however, some cold consolation in the fact that the Americans, who can make so many things, could not make fine steamers, but had to come and buy British boats at a sum altogether out of proportion to their actual value. There was a natural dread that this great American syndicate would "scoop " the Atlantic trade. But the great Cunard Line— undoubtedly the finest line in the world— held out from being nobbled by American dollars into the American syndicate, and was rewarded by the promise of a fine subsidy from the British Government on condition that they built steamers which would outrival any which now run from Queenstown to Sandy Hook. This decisive action upset the inten- tion of the syndicate to control the shipping between the two countries. A great American Tobacco Trust attempted to grab the British trade, and almost succeeded in doing so by the purchase of several of our great tobacco manufacturing firms. The other firms, how- ever, combined, and routed the Americans in so far that instead of the American syndicate sweeping the field they were obliged to go to the English syndicate and plead an amicable working arrangement. Therefore, though only a few months ago British manufacturers resented being described- as behind .the times, and British workpeople were inclined to accuse anyone of a want of patriotism in saying the Americans were quicker than themselves, they have at last realised the situation; and though thq year closes with some gloom in the commTercial world there shifies through it the very brightest of i rays, namely, that the Briton s determined to equip himself for the great struggle for trade, and go through the struggle with the finest machinery, and a readiness to adopt all honourable means which spell for success.

===== 0002601_19030102_art0111.txt | included=true | included =====
THE DEAD.

A sheaf of distinguished men have been garnered in to death. Perhaps the most famous man who passed away was Mr. Cecil Rhodes, who died while the work of his life was only half complete. Naturally there are differences of opinion about the man and his methods, but there can be none about his vigorous personality, indomitable energy, and ceaseless zeal in strengthening the bulwarks of the British Empire. There was something of Napoleon in him—in his austerity, his impatience of restraint, his large grasp of things. More than any man he developed South Africa, and the country which bears his name—Rhodesia—will, if half the glamorous reports be true, prove one of the most valuable tracts within the bounds of the Empire. Also there has passed away the Marquess of Dufferin, perhaps the most successful of British Ambassadors within the last genera- tion, a man who never let loose a grip on British interests, IN ho could be as unyielding as steel, but who owed his long series of successes to a suave courtesy which had much of old-time courtliness about it. The Liberal party lost Lord Kimberley, who was the leader of the little band of Liberal peers in the House of Lords. Lord Pauncefote died at Washing-ton whilst Ambassador to the United States. He was urbane, and never did or said anything to wound the tender suscepti- bilities of our American cousins.

Sir Frederick Abel, who invented cordite, and Herr von Krupp, the great German gnu maker, were a distinct loss to the science of arms. The great apostle of realism, Emile Zola, came to a sad end by being accidentally asphyxiated; Bret Harte, who for a genera- tion delighted the world with his stories of Californian mining life, died in England; and G. A. Henty, whose stories of war and adven- ture were the joy of every British boy, died, as he had often hoped he would do, at sea, on board his yacht: _ . - - - .

In the religious world several striking personalities have been lost. The Rev. Hugh Price Hughes, a man of virility, great power of organisation, and who was earnest in all things and Dr. Parker, a vehement, dramatic, outspoken preacher, were great losses to the, Nonconformists. Also there died Dr. Talmage, who, next to Henry Ward Beecher, was the most popular divine in America; Dr. Newman Hall; and Mr. Kensit, who to the last was the sturdy antagonist of all practices savouring of the Roman Church being intro- &lced into the Church of England.

===== 0002601_19030102_art0112.txt | included=true | included =====
MISCELLANEOUS,

Under this head what a mass and variety of things are to be included! Scientifically, perhaps the chief interest has been in airships. Slowly but surely M. Santos-Dumont in France, and Mr. 'Stanley Spencer in England, have made distinct improvements in the steering of airships. Signor Marconi has thoroughly proved the advantage of wireless telegraphy. The days of the ocean cables will soon be numbered, and yet it has only been this year that there has been completed an all-British cable right round the world. Automobilism, much favoured by the King, is becoming more and more popular. In travel, the Swedish explorer, Sven Hedin, has returned from three years of adventure in Asia, during which he twice attempted to enter the sacred city of Lhassa in Thibet, but unsuccessfully. Nothing outstanding in the way of literature has been produced, one reason probably being that publishers hesitated about bringing out their best during the many months there was comparatively little interest in anything but the Coronation. The same remark almost applies to the drama, though two charming plays, "Mice and Men," by Madeline Lucette Riley, and "Quality Street," by J. M. Barrie, have had much favour—sweet plays which show how far we have travelled in a year or two from sordid, sex, neurotic problem plays. A coal war has been waged in the United States—indeed, the greatest mining strike in the world. It was on the point whether employers should recognise the trade-unions. The conflict in Pennsylvania was bitter and fierce, and there was bloodshed. In the end the men practically won. Not only on this matter, but on the question of trusts, has the United States been gravely agitated, and there is a growing feeling that the law should prevent these gigantic combinations. France has given us the greatest fraud of a octnttauxiny ed,thatniillioofnstbbeyHupmlabnesritbsie astfoarnileialy wanhdo forgeries and who lived in most fashionable style. Many prominent Frenchmen had their names implicated in one way or another with the frauds, and it is generally understood the reason the Humberts—who, of course, flew from France—have not been arrested, is the desire not to drag these names into the mire. With ourselves, we have had a long series of libel actions. The two cases, however, which aroused keenest public interest had to Clo with people in social circles. Mrs. Penruddocke, belonging. to a well-known Wiltshire family, was fined £5O for heartless cruelty to her own child, and there was a storm of indignation. that Mr. Justice Bigham did not send her to prison. The other case was the Hartopp divorce action, in which Sir Charles Hartopp sought divorce from his wife, daughter of Mr. Charles Wilson, M.P., on the ground of misconduct with Earl Cowley. There were countercharges against Sir Charles. None of the charges or countercharges were proved, and the various suits were dismissed. But the trial left a very nasty taste in the public mouth. ln cricket, our AuStralian visitors this year made a splendid show, and won the test match with England. In rating, Sceptre has been the horse of the year, having won the Two Thousand, the One Thousand, the St. Leger, a id the Oaks, and bringing to her ownex, 31r. it S. Sievier, something like i:23,000.

Since the above article was written There have been the additional deaths of the Archbishop of Can- terbury the Bishop of St Albans, and the Dean of Winchester. The Humbert family has also been tracked to Madrid, arrested on December 20th, and siuce taken back to France.

SECONDHAND POLICE TROUSERS.

===== 0002601_19030102_art0114.txt | included=true | included =====
A Lymm Bricklayer's Order.

At Altrincham on Monday, Charles Gettins, of Charles- street, Blackpool, was charged with having obtained 3s. 6d from William Cowsill, bricklayer Lymm, on the 28th November. The case against the defendant was that he advertised in various papers, offering secondhand police trousers at 2s. 6d. to 3s. 6d, per pair, and in response to this advertisement Cowsill sent 3s. 6d. but alleged that he had never received the goods. Inspector H. E. Derham, of the Blackpool police,sttated that since December, 1899, to the present date they had received 324 letters of complaint from all parts of the country, complaining that Gettins had not forwarded the trousers according to arrangement. In cross-examination by Mr. M`Keevor, counsel for the defence, he admitted that the defendant was appar- ently carrying on a bona-fide business in Blackpool. He had been proceeded against at Chesterfield on a similar charge, but this had been dismissed. Police-constable Myatt stated thathe received Gettins noet into custody, and in reply to the charge he said he would be guilty of such a thing, and that the trousers had n forwarded a fortnight ago to Cqwsill. Mr. M`Keevor called the defendant, who said that he °ought large quantities of these secondhand goods, and advertised extensively. He employed two tailors and a tajloress, and his wife also assisted him in the business. rie had executed 2.000 orders during the last fourteen months, but on some occasions goods had gone wrong, and it was most difficult to trace them. Had Cowsill written to him he would most certainly have forwarded another pair. Mrs. Gettins stated that she had put the order on the file, and that it had been duly executed. They were sent by Parcel Post generally. They had, however, no record in the case of those parcels from the Post Office Showing that they had been sent. Mr. Armitage, referring to the number of complaints of non-receipt of parcels, said it was impossible for all these to have got wrong in the post. Mr. 11,1`Keever pointed out that there was no evidence the part of the prosecution to show that one had been lost. On the application of Superintendent O'Kell the case ra-s adjourned for a fortnight, defendant being released In his own recognisances of £5O.

At length they swung roumd the sharp turn, which led to the house, in a most alarming manner, only just missing the gatepost by an inch or two. "Gently, George! Gently round the corner, old man," murmured Bill. George grinned. "What I Haven't you got the veins neither?" he said. _

IN charming indecision she gazed at the two por- traits. The uncertainty in her heart clouded her eyes just enough to of ten and render more be- witching their splendour. "Which shall it be?" she murmured. From one to the other of the pictures her glance wandered. NOW her counte- nance would for a moment beam with the joy of a settled choice, only to darker. again in a moment with perplexity. "If I could only say which one I" A sigh fluttered within the tender bosom and escaped from the rosy lips. "Willis dark. I by. dark men." She clasped her hands, and rap- turously scrutinised the nearest portrait. "But Henry is noble and good." A faint flush crept into her cheeks. Her thoughts were with the et'net picture. "One of them I must marry." Rising to her feet, she paced the floor ner- vously. It was a life's happiness that hung trembling in the balance. "1 am sure both nf them love me with all their hearts." Eland was pressed in hand, until the blood forsook the taper fingers with the frantic pressure. "Both of them—but stay 1" It was a startled look that flashed across her face. "Yes, yes, it is surely so." Belief succeeded anxiety, and a smile of sweet content fluttered about her lips. "If I marry Will, Henry"—She leaned forward fondly towards the portraits. "Henry will give me a wedding present. If I marry Henry, I just know Will would be huffy and never give me a thing." She pondered a moment in nlence. "Yes," she softly whispered at last, "I shall marry Will." High resolve invested het "ersonalitv with a new interest.

The efforts of the Blackburn magistrates to reduce the number of licensed houses in the congested areas of the borough have been successful. There have been several conferences between the justices and brewers and owners of licensed premises, and, as the result of these, a scheme has been drawn up which, when con- firmed by the general body of magistrates, will provide for the extinction of many licences in the more densely crowded districts of the town.

Gartons Limited, Warrington. On Tuesday evening, the 23rd just, Messrs. Garton.; employees held their (inaugural) annual dinner at the Pelican Hotel, Buttermarket-street, when about 30 sat down to dinner. After" having done ample justice to the excellent menu provided by the genial host, Mr. W. Chalmers, Mr. T. R. Garton one of the principals of the firm, o ccupied the chair when a most interest- ing programme was gone through. Carter; the Manager, by Alr. H. New; the Secretary, by Mr. W. C. Duncan; Agri- cultural Interests, by Mr. W. Lowe (of Chester), res- ponded to, by Mr. F, Davies ; Town and Trade of Warrington, by,. BENEFIT

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

Authors of letters intended for publication must in all cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on one side only of the paper and sent in, if possible,not later than Thursday. Correspondents are strongly advised to write briefly. A short letter has a much greater chance of insertion than a long one. We cannot under ordinary circumstances undertake to return the nxanuscripts which are not used.

===== 0002601_19030102_art0145.txt | included=true | included =====
The Recent Eisteddfod.

Sir,—We must again ask your, permission to reply as I briefly as possible to the correspondence on the above. Mr. Payton says we have evaded the main issue. We, however, deait with his letter point by point and evaded nothing. The main issues with us.and which we clearly stated,were the allegations against ourselves. Note how mild he has become. On again referring to his previous letter we find he set out to show .'Why we didn't win," or rather "WhY we were notplacedhigher;" made certain allegations against ourselves and asked us a certain question all of which we sufficiently answered. He seems to have forgotten most of these things, and no* it is merely a question of principle. It is quite clear Mr. Payton is getting over the deep disappdintment of defeat and is sorry he spoke. He cannot, however, get quite over it all at once and en- deavours to give us a fresh dig or two. Mr. Payton should,however, not indulge in newspaper correspond- ence unless he can be more careful. Take the sentence in which he says the Apollo committee "ought not to have exercised any voice in arranging the different pegs for the choirs to hang on." This can be construed in different ways. We are sure,however, that the reputa- tion of the gentleman who acted as judge stands too high in Mr. Payton's eyes for us to take it any way but the most charitable one and we therefore take it that this is intended to be humorous. He then goes on to say in speaking of Mr. Berrey considering he luid a great deal td do with "getting up the Eistedd- fod," Now Mr. Payton should not make statements he ' cannot prove and we defy him or anyone else to prove that remark true. Mr. Berrey had no more to do with getting up the Eisteddfod than had Mr. Payton. The last paragraph is not worthy of a gentleman of Mr. Payton's standing. Perhaps we ought to make plain to the public one thing in our last letter that should be quite clear to every competitor, i.e, when we remarked that we were under no obligation to make inquiries, etc. The rules provided for a protest and any competitor aggrieved had his remedy. Both sides would have been heard and each protest thrashed out on its merits, and if any com- petitor had not got fair play then would have been the time to have exposed us in the press. This straightfor- ward course does not seem to have been to the liking of these babies in competition fighting, and the irrelev- ancies which the letters in last Saturday's paper have developed are ample proof that this correspondence is not bona-fide. Instruction has been freely imparted to the com- mittee as to what they should do by gentlemen who /show by their own letters that they are unable to appreciate the difficulties they are discussing. Just to instance one difficulty upon which we express no opinion but npon which there are many divergent ones. The Warrington Male Voice Choir possess amongst them several trained singers who are constantly paid for their vocal services. Some of these blatant amateurs (?) who appeared in last Saturday's paper might say that these people should have been excluded. We say to any- one holding this opinion or in anyway aggrieved, have the pluck to make and prove your own protest. If you don't, don't come and hit us in the back after it is all over, and at the same time shield yourself behind a non-de-plume. Just a word to those anonymosities who have used the word "Gentlemanly, ungentlemanly, and spirit of generosity." Regarded in the light of the letters in which they appear these words are so much insincere twaddle, and won't prevent us hitting back when we are attacked.

There is another question that is quite off the point and is only further proof of what we have referred to above, that this correspondence is not bona-fide, and springs from petty jealousy and a desire to rehabilitate their own reputation at our expense by detracting from the merit of our victories by every means in their power. For the sake of our members, we cannot, although so contemptibly false, allow it to pass in silence. Are these anonymous individuals who filled almost a column of the " Examiner " so ignorant of their subject that they are unaware that many competi- tions are thrown" open to all corners" (these last four words are from a quotation from a syllabus before me as I write of one of the Eisteddfods where we have won.) We did exactly as our fellow competitors did, and were entitled to do, and is it conceivable that these gentlemen wish the public to believe that such old and experienced competition fighters as Wigan,, Southport, Garswood Src., allowed us without a protest to carry off prizes if we had done anything but what was absolutely fair and square, and in accordance with the rules; and is it possible that they give your readers so little credit for common sense as to try and make them believe that we passed off as an amateur, the best known figure (in Mr. Berrey) in competitions in Lancashire to-day? We have taken a legal opinion on " Interested's" letter and are advised that it is distinctly libellous and that we could sue, but as we know the individual we know consequently that the game would not be worth the candle, but if that indivi- dual has the spirit of a man let him come forth, append his name to his letter and the public will then see who he is and appreciate his motive and remarks at their true worth. .

We are sorry to take up so much of your space Mr. Editor, but we are desirous of keeping our end up our- selves without the assistance of several deputies. Again thanking you in anticipation, we remain yours sincerely, THE COMMITTEE OF THE APOLLO CHOIR. R. W. Cook (Hon. Sec.) 31, Selby-street, Warrington.

GREAT SANKEY MATTERS.

Extra Postal Facilities to be Obtained.

Another Constable Wanted.

Mr. G. Thompson presided at a meting of the Great Sankey Parish Council which was held in the Board schools on Tuesday evening. There were also present :—Messrs. T. Halton, J. Fulton Smith, W. Taylor and J. Smith (clerk).

===== 0002601_19030102_art0147.txt | included=true | included =====
SWINE REMOVAL ORDERS.

The Clerk reported that in accordance with the in- structions of the Council he wrote to, Police Super- intendent Cocker, pointing out that an alteration had recently been made whereby persons removing swine had to make a journey to Earlestown to obtain removal orders. Previously the orders could be obtained from the sergant at Sankey. The journey to Earlestown in- volved considerable loss of time and the previous arrangements would be of greater advantage in every. way. He also wrote to Mr. J. W. Knowles, the repre- sentative of the district on the County Council, calling his attention to the matter. He had received a reply from the Acting Superintendent at Leigh stating that the sergeant at Sankey granted removal orders for swine in the district and it was only necessary to go to the inspector at Earlestown when swine were brought into the district from another local authority,say for instance, the County of Chester. Mr. Knowles replied to the same effect. Mr. Halton, who had introduced the matter, expressed his satisfaction at the result of the correspondence.

===== 0002601_19030102_art0148.txt | included=true | included =====
FURTHER POSTAL FACILITIES TO BE OBTAINED.

The Clerk said he wrot• to the Postmaster at War- rington respecting a Sunday delivery of letters for Hood- lane and Huntley-street, Sankey Bridges, and also with a view to an arrangement being made to enable residents beyond the point at which the delivery would termin- ate to obtain their letters by calling for them on Sun- day morning, say during one hour at the Sankey Bridge's' Post Office, included in the arrangement the residents inCow-lane. He received a reply from the authorities at London stating that from inquiries which had been made it was found that the cost of establishing a 'Sunday service to Great Sankey—with a delivery in Hood-lane and Huntley-street—would be warranted. Before it could be instituted, however, it would be necessary for the Council to pass a resolution in favour of the proposed 'service by a majority of not less than two-thirds of those present, and to give an assurance that it represented the wishes of the inhabitants gener- ally. Mr. Halton moved that the Council, ask the Postmaster General to extend to the residents of Hood- lane a delivery of letters on Sunday morning. He thought that in a growing district such as that it was quite reasonable to ask that they should have a Sunday morning delivery. I Mr. Thompson seconded, and said he was sute it was I the wish of the vast majority of the residents to have I a Sunday delivery. lz was a most important district, and it was rather a hardship that letters could be de- -1 livered in Liverpool-road and not in Hood-lane, where I I the residents had to wait un•01 Monday morning..

Mr. Halton embraced in his resolution that he resi- dents beyond the point at which the delivery would terminate could call at tne Sankey Bridges Office for , letters on the Sunday. This was agreed to, and the Clerk was instructed to give the necessary assurance to the postal authorities.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS.

Rugby Football.

By " Ovalist."

Warrington to-day start a new page in their fixture list, and it is to be hoped that the record to be inscribed upon it,will compare more than favourably with the one just completed. Half the season has gone and the position of the Warrington Club has to be reckoned from the bottom of the table, from which unenviable position they are Seven places removed. That seems fairly good, but the three nearest clubs are very near indeed and might easily profit by a further slip on Warrington's part. As a rule Warrington are higher up on the table than this—and they may reasonably hope to rise in the world during the next four months, for the fixtures are mostly at home. Last season at this time Warrington stood third in the League table' only BroughtOn Rangers and Hull beinc, above them. This was mainly due, however, to the success which attended the team in the holiday matches, when Huddersfield, Leigh, and Runcorn were successively vanquished. The results have been just the opposite this time, and Warrington have lost every point during the holidays. That there is better luck in store for the club I hope and believe.

TM last match of 1902 was with Huddersfield. on Saturday at Fartown.. Warrington were well repre- sented and had reasonable prospects of victory, but success was denied them. Truth to tell they did not deserve it. Deere got two tries on Fish's wing which Ido not think he would have got had the County left-winger been in condition for the game. By the way he moved, Christmas plum pudding seemed to lie heavy on Fish's chest. His lack of form was mainly responsible for the defeat, the other members of the team giving a fair display. There was never- theless none of that dashing combination which one was wont to see from the Warrington backs when at full, strength. Such combination as there was, was inefficient and slow,the half-backs failing conspicuously to get the ball clear of the nick. Hockenhall frequently ran too far diagon- ally and he should watch this point carefully for it spoils which would otherwise be a promising opening. Dickenson and Isherwood both strove manfully to break through,but had no luck and the Fartowners wen by eight points to five.

Warrington's try was only (Tot in the last minute of the game when Edmunds ran down from Dickenson's pass and centred so cleverly that Roberts had the line at his mercy. He dropped on the ball under the goal and Fish popped it over the bar. Deere's two tries demonstrated that he is a flier, and the fact that he should have been stopped does not detract from the merit of his performances.

The Huddersfield team is one of the very few which consistently play open football. When they came to Warrington last season this fact was remarked, and on Saturday last they maintained their reputation. Up to the time that the home team scored the second try the game was very open, and full of combined move- ments which carried the ball from one end of the field to the other. In the latter stages it deteriorated some- what, but as a whole it was a very pleasant game to watch. Both Deere's tries were the outcome of good passing and the same may be said for the one which fell to Warrington. On the Huddersfield side both Dewhirst and Deere were very good on the right wing the other pair being only moderate. Readyhough was in fine form at full-back and quite outclassed .Hallam who was very unsteady and slow. Among an ener- getic and medium-weight pack of forwards Chambers stood out conspicuously. lam told he is a Methodist parson and, of course, an amateur. If he is anything like so good a preacher as he is a forward he should be worth listening to. In their search for forwards the Warrington Committee have probably never thought of looking through the ministerial ranks I'll warrant, Could not some bulky parson be persuaded to don the "primrose and blue.

have already referred to the play cif some of the Warrington men. The experiment on the right wing was of course, keenly Watched. Edmunds did very well for his inches,but there is no gainsaying the fact that his smallness renders him incapable of filling a regular place in first league football. He is a clever little player, and it is a great pity that Nature has not blessed him with a little more length and breadth. The forwards gave a fair display and they could not, on this occasion be blamed more than anyinie else kr the defeat.

Broughton Rangers on Saturday lowered the colours of the Halifax men and now stand an even better chance than the latter of securing the champ;on- ship. Swinton failed at Hull by a goal-kick, while Runcorn did a good performance in winning at Wigan. Perhaps they were lucky! Anyhow they have decided not to report the Wigan crowd for the mobbing which followed the nne-point victory. Widnes and St. Helens had a deadly duel at Widnes, where the home side won comfortably.

MR. JOSEPH HARRISON, IP.

DEATH AND FUNERAL. A Long Public Career.

In the early hours of Sunday morning, there peace- fully passed away Mr. Joseph Harrison, J.P., at the advanced acre of 80 years. He died at his residence, Woodbine 'Terrace, Latchford, and the immediate cause Of death was cirrhosis of the spinal cord. Mr. Harrison's long career was closely identified with the public life of Warrington. Up to within a few years ago, when failing health confined him to the house,the late Mr. Harrison took a prominent part in the municipal, political and philan- throllic wprk of the town. A Warrington man by birth, he took an interest in all its affairs and assisted in its many activities. To a man who had led such an active life, it was a great trial to be confined to his bedroom for the last two years oF his life, but he bore it with great fortitude and patience. Mr. Charles White was his medical attend- ant, and was unremitting in his attention to relieve the suffering of his patient. On Friday night, Mr. Harri- son was in an unconscious condition, and it was seen that the end was very near. He gradually sank, and breathed his last at four o'clock on Sunday morning. He was the eldest son of the late Mr. William Harrison, the first appointed registrar of Warrington, and a member of the family of Harrisop, of "Pill Moss," Over Whitley, Cheshire—a family of the Yeomanly class who owned a small estate there for several generations. He had been twice married; the first time to Emma Sarah the youngest daughter of the late Mr. James Charles, of Mosley-street, Manchester, of the firm of Hague Marshall, and • Tidswell, calico printers; and the ' second time to Bessie, second daughter of Mr. Charles Atkinson, land surveyor,, of Lincoln. He is survived by his widow and a son.

For more. than 30 years, the late Mr. Harrison was High Bailiff of the Warrington County Court, with which he had been connected since its establishment in 1846. During that titne three judges and three registrars had been in office. When Mr. Harrison resigned his position in 1897, the duties were under- taken by the Registrar, Mr. T. J. Ridgway. Mr. Harrison first entered the Town Council in , November 1864 as the representative of the South West Ward, now known as St. Austins Ward, and it was not until the 9th November, 1900, that he te-- minated his connection with the Corporation after 36 years continuous service. During that time he had sat on. all the committees of the Corperation,had been pro- moted to an Aldermanic seat, had been thrice elected Mayor, and finally in 1897 the honorary freeman of the Borough was conferred upon him" in recognition of his eminent public services." During his Mayoralty in the years 1883-4-5-6, Mr. Harrison instituted the Christmas hot-pot for the poor, an institution which has been maintained up to the present, and one which is greatly appreciated by the poorest of the town. Mr: Harrison was an accomplished speaker, and his speeches were frequently interlarded with quotations from: Shakespeare. Mr. Harrison was appointed a justice of tha peace for the Borough by the Crown in 1886, and as long as he was able to climb the steps leading to the Court Room at the old Police Station, he was very regular in his attendance on the Bench. In giving the deci- sions of the Bench he invariably delivered a little homily to the defendaict or the prisoner, which was sometimes appreciated but more frequently not. For several years he was chairman of the Board of Guar- dians, and for a considerable period a member, and he was quite an authority on poor-law administration, of which he had made a careful study. He also greatly interested himself in the Infirmary and Dis- pensary, of which he was a life governor, and one of his last public appearances was at a meeting of the Governors of this institution, three years ago.

VOTE OF CONDOLENCE. Speaking at the annual meeting of the Warrington Conservative Club, on Tuesday evening, Alderman Francomb observed that since their last meeting they had heard of the death of a very old and respected member of the Conservative Association, namely, Mr. Joseph Harrison, J.P. They all knew what a staunch Conservative he had been and what a treie and honour- able supporter of the cause he was. No man had evey lived in Warrington who had worked more loyally for the party. He had lived to a ripe old age, over 80years, but still they were exceedingly sorry to bear of his death. He had known the time when Mr. Harrison was a mighty power in Warrington, but time was the grent leveller of all of them; no matter how strong a man was in his youth, age put an end to that strength, and the grave finished them all off. He moved that a resolu- tion of sympathy be sent from the meeting to Mrs Harrison and family. Mr. THOMAS MASON, in seconding the proposition, observed that he was sure he was speaking the senti- ments of all present when he said they fully reciprocated the kind remarks which Alderman Francomb had made. The Conservatives had lost a gteat friend in the late Mr. Harrison, who took a deep and active interest in everything that appertained to the welfare of the Con- servative party. He was always found ready to render assistance. They sincerely sympathised with Mrs. Har- rison in the great affliction which had befallen her. The motion was unanimously carried.

WINWICK ASYLUM HOSPITAL A CCOMMODATION. THE TREATMENT OF SMALL-PDX CASES AT SANKEY. Dr. SEPHTON reported that during the month there had been 26 births and 36 deaths, the death-rate being equal to 37 per 1,000. Two deaths from ent eric fever had occurred in Winwick Asylum, and five fresh cases had been notified. As requested by the Council he had made inquiries with regard to the isolation hospital at Winwick Asylum. He had ascertained that the Local Government Board required that every building which was to contain infected persons or things should be at least forty feet distant from the asylum boundary. As the building in question was fully tha.t distance from the boundary the Cornea had no power to move in the matter. Eighteen of the deaths notified occurred in the asylum, and exclusive of these the death rate was 17.8. He bad received a letter dated December 29th, from Dr. Sergeant, County Medical °Meer, with regard to typhoid fever at Winwick Asylum, stating that no case had occure,d during the past sixteen days. He had care7ully investigated the outbreak and considered that personal infection from an introduced ambulatory case was the most likely cause of the spread of the disease. Precautions were being taken in every direction, and at the present time the drains were being inspected by an experienced sanitary engineer. Mr. HALTON said he naderstood that a case of small- pox had been removed to Sankey. Could the medical officer or inspector give him any information with regard to the drainage of the building in which the patient was confined.

The INSPECTOR said the sewnge ran into a ditch as was the case in most country places. ALLEGED THEFT AT EARLESTOWN.

At the Warrington Occasional Court on Monday, John Keenan, a young man, was remanded to the Warrington County Petty Sessions.on the charge of stealing a vest and muffler, value 55., the property of John Walters. an Earlestown collier, on October 21st.—Evidc.nce was given to the effect that prisoner went to lodge at prose- cutor's house, but left the following morning, and after his vest and muffler were missed. WA.RGRAVE CHURCH CHOIR SOCIAL.

The great Christmas event at Burton Wood is the Mercy Tree. The tree is anticipated weeks beforehand with great delight by the children and young people of the parish. Saturday afternoon last saw a packed tea- room, and,later, a very crowded tree and party room. The company, including adults, numbered about 400, and provision had been made for the free gifts to some 300 children young and old. The evening was a lively one, and gave the guests intense delight. The Vicar presided, Mrs. Webster distributed the Merciful League rewards.

THE NEW YEAR AT EARLESTOWN AND NEWTON.

With the closing of the various works, and the exodus of a large number of people bent on holiday making the streets wore a very quiet appearance. The night of New Year's Eve was fine, but beyond pedestrians wending their way to the Watchnight Services at the various places of Worship, there were not many people about. On the stroke of 12 o'clock, the birth of a new year was heralded by the blowing of the buzzers at the different works in the neighbourhood, but there was an entire absence of rowdyism. As inprevious years the Viaduct brass band mustered on the Market-square, and dis- coursed several selections of appropriate music. Here also a large number of people, chiefly of the younger generation congregated, and as the New Year was entered upon they enjoyed themselves in dancing and singing topical songs. New Year's morning was notable for a thick fog, but this happily cleared away about ten o'clock and a beautiful day supervened. In the afternoon some 2,000 or 3,000 people assembled on the Earlestown football g,round to witness an encounter between Earlestown and bt Helens Town in the Lancashire League the latter winning the rubber by 3 goals to 1, a severe disappointment to the , Earlestown spectators who fully expected their favour- ites to win.

In the evening the various schools held their annual treats, at which there were distributions of awards for punctuality and lessons. At the Wesleyan Sunday school, after tea, the usual annual school report was read and a pleasing entertain- ment was given consisting of vocal items and recita- tions by the Brunswick quartet party, Messrs. G. W. Kidd, R. A. Robinson, J. Eckersley,Mrs.C. W. Hipwood, Misses 0. Booth, Ethel King, Minnie Barr, Eleanor Teece, Alice Green and Master Joseph Atkinson. A humorous dialogue "Six of one and half-a-dozen of the other," was presented, and created much amusement, the characters being represented by Messrs. W. Clare, Theo. Clare S. Gregory, Misses K. Alderson, Marsh and France. The prizes to the children wer e afterwards dis- tributed by the Rev. C. E. 'Dove. The usual gatherings were also held at the Congrega- tional and Baptist Sunday schools whereentertainments were given and awards distributed. At the Primitive Methodist schoolroom the elder scholars held their annual New Year's treat, the evening, after tea, being spent in singing and various games.

ST. HELENS.

A BOY'S DANGEROUS ACT.

William Odom, aged twelve, of Glover-street, St. Helens, was charged at St. Helens on Monday with stealing eleven rubber washers from the vacuum-brake pipes of the London and Nortb-Western Railway passenger coaches at St. Helens Railway Station on the 19th and 20th inst. Peter Harrison, foreman carriage cleane7, spoke to missing the rubber washers, and said that if one of the vehicles was placed in a train tho brakes would not have acted properly. There was con- siderable danger.of something serious happening.—The magistrates cautioned the lad as to his future conduct and ordered him to receive six strokes with the birch. ST. HELENS COLLIERY BOY'S STRANGE ACCIDENT.

An inquest was held at Peasley Cross, St. Helens, on Saturday afternoon, on Harold Forster, aged thirteen, son of the "underlooker" at the King Pit of the Sutton Heath and Lea Green collieries, who died in the St. Helens Hospital on Christmas Day morning. The lad hurt himself while jumping off a stage in the haulage road ol the pit on November 14th. There were no marks of injury, and he worked again on the following day (Saturday), although he was in pain. He resided at the Nutgrove Colliery-yard. Dr. Jackson subsequently found that the bottom of his backbone had been Nu, ed, and at the hospital performed an operation, but the lad sank and died. De. Jackson told the jury that by some strange chance the lad had broken a piece of bone off the inner Fart or front side of the backbone,and this had caused an immense amount of suppmation. The boy died from exhaustion. A verdict of "Accidental death "was returned.

HANS GROWYOST, a worthy Dutch farmer, has a shrewish wife, who has long rendered his life any- thing but a couch of roses. One day, not long ago, she in a fit of pique packed up her goods and left him, vowing never to come back. The news went round among his neighbours, and at night several of them came to condole with Hans. He sat on his front post pulling away at his pipe. "Hans," remarked one of his neighbours, I pity you." "My friend," replied the honest Dutchman, as ho knocked disturbedly the ashes out of his pips, "you vas right. She vas shust come back."

ANNUAL MEETING OF THE

WARRINGTON CONSERVATIVE CLUB.

The (annual meeting of the Warrington Conservative. Club was held on Tuesday evening. Mr. Alderman Francomb presided and there were also present: Coun- cillors W. J. Forshaw, John Roberts, John Pemberton, Messrs. W. W. Westwell, C. Leigh, T. Mason, T. H. Ashcroft, W. B. Houghton, A. Carruthers, W. Bindloss, W. H. Bellian, G. Jolley, W. Wilson. W. Holbrook, H. Collin, John Cox, J. Strettle, R. Chadwick, with G. Pilling (secretary), W. Owen (treasurer) and R. H. Dainteth (assistant secretary.)

THE ANNUAL REPORT. The HON. SECRETARY in submitting the 18th annual report said the committee had thuch pleasure in report- ing that the membership of the club was still,well main- tained, but were still of opinion that there were many Conservatives in the town who if they were approached' would become members. The club durinf, the last year hacl been well attended and had been conducted by the members in every way that could be desired, and every- thing in:the way of tables and general condition of the premises were satisfactory. The annual ball took place. in February in the -Parr Hall and was well attended Their thanks were due to those gentlemen who undertook to Carry out the arrangements. The bowling green ha a been well attended during the seaspn, the condition of which had been very good, the usual mains for prizes having taken place at the opening and closing of the same. The annual billiard handicaps took place when a large number of members entered. Their thanks were due to those who subscribed to the prize fund. The Committee were of opinion that it was through these healthy and enjoyable games that the membership of the Club was so well kept up, _

The HON TREASURER said his financial statement was something like a,Christinits pantomine "Hear we are again." (Laughter.) And they had been very success- ful during the year, and they had money in hand. The club had been kept up in every way. He had the pleasure the other day of taking a gentleman from Lancaster through it and be praised it beyond measure. He hoped the club had been maintained to their satis- faction. They had plenty of room for more first and second class members. (Hear, hear.) The reports were adopted. ALDERMAN FRANCOMBS' OPINION OP THE CLUB. The CHAIRMAN said he believed the club was kept in as good a position RS it was possible to he. They had not seen the slightest appearance of anyone "getting over the line" with drink, and he believed that the games engaged in, were such games as tended to sober- ness and not to drinking. (Hear, hear.) • They could_ ask any clergyman, Roman Catholic priest, or Dissent- ing minister to visit the buildtng. If they did they would not go away with a wrong impression. He (the Chairman) was not here at the last hour every night,but he was constantly here until ten o'clock, and he had never seen anything that was improper. They had recre- ation in the form of billiards and bowls, but they would put their feet down on anything like gambling. If anyone attempted to do anything in the shape of gambling he believed there was sufficient moral strength" amongst the members to put a stop to it at once. (Hear, hear.) He thought the members vied with each other to keep the club in such a condition that it was a credit to the town and the association. Councillor JOHN ROBERTS did not think there was a better managed club in existence. They wanted men of a good stamp—not Toni, Dick or Harry who came simply for the purpose of drinking and gambling. (Hear,, , hear.)

COMPLAINT AS TO VENTILATION. Mr. CARRUTHERS drew dttention to the condition of the second class room which, he said, required ventilat- ing. He suggested the introduction of an electric fan. Mr. PILLING promised that the matter should be taken. into consideration by the committee. THE COMMITTEE. The following were appointed the members of the committee :—Messrs. R. W. Francomb, John Roberts,. George Pilling, James Strettle, Gorge Jolley, Robert Chadwick, John Cox, W. H. Bellian, Charles Leigh,. Charles McDowell, and Daniel Cooper. ELECTION OF OFFICERS. Mr. George Pilling was unanimously re-elected hon. secrete ry and Mr. William Owen, hon. treasurer. THE CHAIRMAN AND THE TWO POLITICAL CLUBS. Councillor FORSHAW, in proposing a vote of thanks; to the chairman, said he had discharged his duties in a very able and admirable manner, and their best thanks were to him. (Hear, hear.) Mr. LEIGH seconded, and the motion was carried. In reply, Alderman FRANCOMB said, that one of the greatest pleasures of his life was to be associated with the club with which he had been connected for over 25 years. He had been chairman fora great number of years, ever since the death Of their dearly beloved friend Captain Reynolds. He was proud of the club, because he believed it was conducted on good lines. He had known the Liberal Club for 20 years, and he had never heard of anything wrong there. He had a strong belief that •if' the leaders of the two political parties would visit the clubs occasionally—(hear, hear)—and mix with themem- bers, it would do no harm and the leaders would lose no 'prestige. (Hear. hear.)

THE NEW LICIacSING ACT. The following were appointer* licensing committee to consider the new Licensing Act as it will affect clubs :---Messrs. J. Strettle, Charles Leigh, V 1L hellion, W. W. Westwell, Thomas Mason, W. J. Wilson, and W. J. Forshaw. This was all the business. A reference to the late Mr. Joseph Harrison will be found in another column.

Accident at the Vulcan Foundry.

At about 3 o'clock on Monday afternooTt a man named Hugh McDonald, a labourer at the Vulcan Foundry, met w ith an accident which necessitated his removal to the Warrington Infirmary. McDoeald was engaged in the pulling down of an old shed, when an iron girder which had been insecurely fixed fell on him, fracturing his knee. He was conveyed to Dr. Mouncey's surgery, where the injured limb was set, and the horse ambulance was afterwards used for his conveyance io Warrington.

A Huntsman Breaks Three Ribs. The North Cheshire Hunt met at Tarporley on Saturday and scored a brilliant gallop. Hounds drew blank several covers, and-the fortunes of the chase had sunk low when the Master turned for a fox to Calveley Gorse, a cover which has latterly failed to justify its good name. On this occasion it redeemed itself by yielding one of the best foxes ever bred there. Hounds at once fastened on to his line, and raced straight away to Oulton Lowe, and going on over the Tarporley-road they just missed the adjuncts as they held forward for -Blakeden. Skirting that well-known cover they continued to run at a great pace for the Forest, and they were just behind their fox when he went to g,round on Mr Holland's farm at Budworth. The gallop was by far the best which has fallen to the North Cheshire Hunt; this season, although it lasted Only twenty-five minutes. It con- tained a good five and a half miles point, and only a very few of the field kept their places until the finish. During a subsequent hunt from Blakeden to Abbots Moss,'Cubberley, the huntsman, had a bad fall, which rendered him unconscious. He was sent home in the matiter's 'brougham, and it was tvn found he had I broken three ribs.

. A Severe 6„,,,,,,,,,,,\ Test. , el e A\ The following ,_.,,.,5) ~ . 11 appeared in the li. ' - ' ritO • 12:2,5.9-4'y • '', 'll " Staffordshire ~ , %, ;,,,, ,ii Sentinel:"— - -. i,_ , ~ 11 Wondersnever , 'T Pki V ,/,' / cease. The other s re ,v% day a football 1 1A 0 vvas put to a , singular, not to say severe, test in a town not a thousand miles from Burslem. Along a lane in which are laid cart rails, some youths were taking a ball, when it was accidently kicked into the rut just as a cart was approaching. The cart contained a heavy load of coal. The wheel passed over the ball, and pressed it down until it was almost flat. The onlookers momentarily expected the dread explosion, but, hey presto! the ball actually lifted the weight up. On examination it was found to be sound, and to even have retained its roundness. Verily football manufacturers would be delighted to receive such testimony as to the durability of their wares." We may add that the ball was none other than the famous "Football King," supplied by Mr. Frank Sugg of Liverpool. FOOTBALL KIM% Post Free, 9/6.

ing reading and should be read by all who are concerned in the town's welfare. Thrift among the workingpeople continues to spread, thanks mainly to Mr. Handley's efforts as actuary. I would call special attention to the balance sheet published in our advertisement columns. As w 11 be seen from an advertisement there is to be an exhibition of students' work and exhibits illustrat- ing the industries of the district in the Technical Insti- tute. Our readers ale advised to see what promises to be one of the most interesting exhibits held in Runcorn for some time. --- The last days of the old year were spent by most of the churches in fitting devotional exercises. At the Parish Church and the St. Paul's Church, special watch- night services were held to speed the parting year and herald the new. As usual the streets were crowded with men and women who behaved in the hilarious manner peculiar to the festive season. At twelve o'clock, 1903 was signalled by the blowing of fog horns, buzzers and whistles. Long into the early morning,groups of revellers made sweet music to the morning air while some bands of carol singers actually warbled forth their praises until morn- ing light. Happily the weather was somewhat mild, it being anything but seasonable. New Year's Day is generally regarded by the tradesmen as aholiday and the streets presented quite a thronged appearance.

Taylor, Heathercliff e, Helsby 25. GIFT OF COAL BY MR. TAYLOR. Mr. Cooke reported that Mr. Taylor, with his usual generosity, had sent through him a hundredweight of coal to 210 needy persons. On the motion of the Chairman, seconded by Mr. Norman, a vote of thanks was passed to Mr. Taylor. The Chairman said that that completed the whole business of the year. He hoped they would continue to meet in harmony to transact their business, and to do right between those they renresented and those they had charge of in the House. Mr. Mosely-Williams, as one of the oldest members, reciprocated the good wishes of the Chairman, and wished him a prosperous New Year.

• Alterations at Warrington Public Houses. At the County Borough Police Court on Monday, before Mr. F. Monks and other magistrates, Mr. Browne applied on behalf of Messrs. Greenall, Whit- ley and Co., for permission to make alterations at the "Black Bear" Inn, Knutsford-road. lie said the drinking area would not be altered in any shape or form. At present there was a stable adjoining the inn, and it was proposed to pull it down and rebuild it at the back of the house. Superintendent Monaghan had seen the plans and had no objection to the appli- cation.—The Bench sanctioned the alterations.—Mr. Browne then made an application for permission to carry out certain alterations in regard to the domestic and sanitary accommodations at the "White Hart" Hotel, Sankey-street, and this was also granted.
